<meta>This page continues the narrative of Tertia Aemilia, the wife of Scipio Africanus, focusing on her reaction to her husband's infidelity in his old age.</meta>

...parents of the Virgin offered for her redemption <note>This opening line appears to be the conclusion of a previous section, possibly comparing the virtues of secular women to religious figures.</note> / he,
having now become old, could not withdraw himself from the damnation of that filth,
vile lust and concupiscence <term>concupiscence: strong sexual desire or lust, often discussed in medieval texts as a spiritual failing</term>, but fell into the affection
and concubinage of one of his female servants. And since it is
a very difficult thing to deceive the knowledge and perception
of honest love, this thing could not be hidden from Tertia
Emilia; rather, in the course of time, she learned the whole matter.
Who would doubt that she bore this thing with little patience?
For many women affirm that, all shame being put aside, nothing more injurious nor less tolerable can be done to a flighty woman of little sense than her bed being granted by her husband to another woman;
which I easily believe. For the woman thinks that her husband
does it to the detriment and diminution of the love he owes her.
Nevertheless, this noble lady bore it with constant courage;
for she hid and concealed <note>original: "muca," a Middle French term meaning to hide or secrete away.</note> that crime of her husband under
such great silence that not only another person,
but even her husband himself could not perceive that she understood
anything of what her said husband had done.

¶ **She thought, however.** <note>original Latin: "Existimabat autem." The translator includes this Latin phrase as a structural marker before expanding in French.</note> ¶

That discreet lady estimated it a most improper and unbecoming thing that it should be known in public that he—who by his noble virtue had subjugated kings and valiant nations—was himself subjugated and vanquished in and by the love of a servant girl. Nor did it seem to that most holy woman Emilia...

<summary>This page describes the stoicism of Tertia Aemilia upon discovering her husband Scipio Africanus's affair with a servant, choosing to hide his indiscretion to protect his public reputation as a conqueror of nations.</summary>

--- Page 2 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous section discussing the virtues of a patient wife—likely the biblical Sarah—who permitted her husband to have children by a servant. It then transitions into Chapter 81, focusing on Hypsicratea.</meta>

...paid the marital duty <note>The "marital duty" refers to the medieval legal and religious concept of the *debitum conjugale*, the obligation of spouses to provide for each other's physical needs, here interpreted as Sarah allowing Abraham to conceive with Hagar.</note> of her husband to her servant-companion who was with her, and who gave up her own portion to them. Because we have seen such a thing happen so rarely, we ought to consider her all the more great and worthy of perpetual renown. Considering that any other woman but this one would have cried out and made a great noise, and would have called her parents, relatives, neighbors, and all the women she knew to a council; and with a long sermon she would have filled and burdened them with innumerable complaints and quarrels. She would have suffered being left, abandoned, and vilified by her husband, and while he lived she would have been cast out like a rejected widow. She would have cast that servant and low-born wanton <term>ribaudelle: a derogatory term for a woman of low status or morals</term> out of the temple—and what is more, would have sold her! She would have publicly attacked and harrassed her husband with cries, feigned tears, and groans; she would not have cared for anything but defending her right by force of words, and in doing so, she would have stained the noble renown of her very honest husband.

She was famous for her masculine courage and devotion to him.</note> of the great Mithridates, King of Pontus. And although the faith by which we are subject and bound to our parents made her commendable...

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Hypsicratea, the loyal wife of King Mithridates VI, and begins the eighty-first chapter concerning the Roman woman Sempronia.</meta>

Nevertheless, in my judgment, Lady Nature made her even more memorable through an unheard-of occurrence. For if faith is to be placed in the books or records of the ancients, this woman, from the hour of her birth, possessed a double row of teeth in her mouth, presenting in her time a sight and spectacle that seemed monstrous to all those of Asia. Even though she was not in any way hindered in drinking or eating, because of that great and well-known deformity—so unusual was it—she was considered horrible and monstrous. 

Nevertheless, through her praiseworthy loyalty, she overcame all the aforementioned deformity. For when her father <note>Hypsicratea was actually the wife of Mithridates VI, though the French translator here uses "pere" (father); Boccaccio's original Latin correctly identifies her as his wife.</note> Mithridates was overcome by the Great Pompey <note>Cnaeus Pompeius Magnus, the Roman general</note>, she followed her said father without sparing herself from any pains, labors, or perils. And by this very faithful service, she bore witness and gave it to be understood that the "crimes" and defaults of nature should be attributed to the father and mother <note>This reflects a medieval belief that physical "monstrosities" were inherited or caused by the parents, yet Hypsicratea's virtue proved her character was her own.</note>.

¶ Chapter 81 treats of Sempronia, a Roman woman. And the text begins: *Sempronia, daughter...* <note>original: "sepzonia filia"</note> etc.

She was the wife of the most noble and resplendent Scipio Aemilianus, who, because of his capture and destruction of Carthage, attained the surname of his grandfather, the first Scipio, and was likewise called Africanus. <meta>This page continues a discussion of the noble character of the Gracchi family, specifically focusing on a female relative—likely Sempronia, the sister of the famous reformers Tiberius and Gaius Gracchus—following the violent deaths of her brothers.</meta>

...Gaius, she <note>The feminine pronoun "laquelle" indicates the text is now describing a woman of the family, historical records identify her as Sempronia, the sister of the Gracchi.</note> in no way degenerated or fell away from the greatness and constancy of courage found in the greatest of her lineage.

After the brothers were slain <note>original Latin: "Post cesos fratres"</note>. After the death of her brothers, on account of their seditions <note>The Gracchi brothers were famous Roman social reformers whose political challenges to the aristocracy led to their assassinations.</note>, it is said to have happened that she was brought into judgment before a <term>tribune of the people</term> <term>Tribune: A powerful Roman official elected to protect the rights of the common citizens and capable of vetoing state actions.</term>. She was summoned and handled there in a manner that caused no small amount of observation and astonishment at her courage. Either by the full power of the tribunate or by the entire multitude favoring the cause, she was most urgently commanded to kiss a man named Equitius, from the land and territory of Pisa, as if he were the son of her brother Tiberius Gracchus, and to receive him as a member of the <term>Sempronian family</term> <term>Sempronian: The "gens Sempronia" was the noble Roman family to which the Gracchi belonged; the crowd wanted her to legitimize an impostor claiming to be her nephew.</term>. 

And although this lady saw herself to be in a place where kings and princes were accustomed to tremble with fear—being exhorted on one side by the diverse and discordant voices of the ignorant crowd, and on the other side threatened by the sovereign authority of the tribunes with terrifying and cruel faces and countenances—nevertheless...

### The 78th chapter treats of Quinta Claudia, which begins in the text: <note>original: "claudia quinta romana fuit"</note> *Claudia Quinta was a Roman woman.*

[Miniature: Two women on a ship, one kneeling in prayer, the other standing. They are on a wooden deck with a mast and rigging. In the background, a landscape with a rocky hill and a blue sky.]

The said Claudia was a Roman woman, though it is not clear enough from which parents she was born. Nevertheless, through a very noble boldness, she acquired perpetual renown. 

This lady was very meticulous in her use of ornaments and rich, constant, and diverse clothing; in her speech and manner, she was very elegant and well-composed. For this reason, she was not considered any less <term>honorable</term><note>The Middle French "honneste" refers to social respectability and "fitting" behavior for her rank.</note> by the most serious and elderly matrons of Rome; however, she was reputed to be far less chaste. Certainly the...

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the arrival of the goddess Cybele (the Great Mother) in Rome in 204 BCE, focusing on the miraculous intervention of the Roman matron Claudia Quinta.</meta>

...arrival, which carried the image <note>original: "simulacre"; this refers to the sacred black stone or statue representing the goddess Cybele</note>; but it happened that the sailors and matrons [and] the whole multitude of young men pulling this ship could not bring it near the shore because of the waves of the river which hindered them; [and] this continued until Claudia, mingled among the other matrons and trusting in her virtues, publicly knelt down, humbly praying to the goddess that if she deemed her to be chaste, she would deign to follow her belt <note>original: "sainture"; a sash or girdle. Claudia Quinta’s reputation for purity had been slandered, and she used this moment as a divine trial of her character.</note> with which she fastened the ropes of the ship; and this was done immediately and in great confidence, hoping that which she had requested would come to pass, and she commanded that all the young men should move away from there. Nor could that ship ever be brought to the bank until Claudia alone pulled it in this way. And indeed, she pulled it to the great admiration and amazement of all [and] led it to the place that she desired.

¶ <note>Latin: "Ex quo tam mirabili," meaning "From which so wonderful [an event]."</note> ¶ From which so marvelous an adventure, it followed immediately that the opinion that almost everyone held—that Claudia had not well guarded her chastity— <note>The text concludes here, implying that the miracle cleared her name.</note>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of a noblewoman—identifiable as Hypsicratea, Queen of Pontus—who transformed her lifestyle to follow her husband, King Mithridates VI, into war.</meta>

...in place of belts and fine fabrics. And she did all these things so fittingly that, from this tender and delicate queen, you would have thought there had been made and become a seasoned and very expert knight. And those things she was accustomed to—such as reclining and staying in royal chambers and palaces, wandering about and being idle, dainty, and delicate without ever venturing out—having been left behind and forgotten by her, she began to ride horses, laden with armor. She ran after her husband through sudden and hasty journeys, through the most harsh and difficult passages of dark mountains and narrow valleys, overcoming all heat and cold without caring for herself or taking any account of her own comfort. 

And very often, in place of her chambers and royal beds, she was constrained by heavy sleep to lie down and fall asleep without having any fear near wild and cruel beasts. And whether her husband was the victor or the vanquished, she was always, and in all places, his praiseworthy company, aid, and help in all his counsels and labors.

<note>original Latin: "Quid multa"</note> Why should I say more? She accustomed and taught her sweet and pitying eyes to be able—without horror or astonishment—to look upon wounds, killings, and the blood of the dead and the injured, which she herself sometimes spilled and caused to flow with the blows of the lance and the sword that she gave. And her ears, accustomed to sweet and melodious songs, she forced to hear the neighing of horses and the tumult and noise of weapons and the blows of those who fought by land and sea, without any wavering of her mind. 

After many labors, pains, and dangers suffered by her—which would have been very heavy even for a robust knight to endure—she followed her said husband when he was vanquished and overcome by Gaius Pompey <note>Pompey the Great, the Roman general</note>. This husband fled with a few of his friends through mountains and narrow passes, through the woods and forests of Armenia, through the valleys and dark, shadowy places of the region of Pontus <note>An ancient kingdom on the southern coast of the Black Sea</note>, and through many unbridled, savage, and cruel nations of people. And sometimes, to her afflicted and sorrowful husband, she gave re<note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "comfort" or "relief"</note>

<summary>This page describes the transformation of Queen Hypsicratea into a warrior-companion to her husband, Mithridates VI, detailing her endurance of physical hardship and her loyalty following his defeat by the Roman general Pompey.</summary>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page concludes the biography of Hypsicratea, Queen of Pontus, and begins the account of the Roman woman Sempronia. Hypsicratea was the devoted wife of King Mithridates VI, famous for following him into exile disguised as a man.</meta>

...creation and the hope of better fortune. In former times, she used to appease and soothe him with comforts and amusements, and by all the things in which she knew he took pleasure. And in effect, she gave him so much consolation that in whatever solitude, or desert land, or strange misery and tribulation he found himself, it seemed to him that he was in his royal chambers and beds, refreshed <note>original: "refocile"; meaning revived or warmed again</note> and comforted with every delight and pleasure.

¶ O most holy heart of marital sweetness! ¶ Virtue of friendship that never fails! Never did a woman suffer more grievous pains for her husband than this lady did; but certainly, for all the labors sustained by this woman on behalf of her husband, she did not receive from him a worthy enough reward <note>original: "retribucion ou louyer"</note>. For as he was in a rage, he killed a fine son of his whom he had fathered with her. And when, by the pressure of the Romans, he had retreated not only into his kingdom but into his castle and royal city, and while he still endeavored to sustain and stir up war against the Romans and distant nations, he was besieged by his son Pharnaces <note>The text spells this "Pharnay." Pharnaces II eventually rebelled against his father, Mithridates.</note>, who rebelled against him because of the cruelty and tyranny he used toward his friends and children. 

This Mithridates, seeing himself thus besieged and finding that by no prayer could he find pity in his son, and sensing the end of the final ruin of all his affairs—so that the noble Hypsicratea, who had previously given him so much aid, consolation, and comfort, should not survive him—he made her, along with his other wives, concubines, and his daughters also, die before him by the venom and poison that he gave to them.

¶ Truly ungrateful. <note>original Latin: "Sane ingratum"</note>

¶ Certainly, the ingratitude of this Mithridates cannot diminish the glory that the said lady, his wife, had deserved through her merits; rather, her name shall live perpetually through noble renown.

[Main body]
of noble lineage and resplendent beauty. [symbol] In her husband as well as in her children, she was quite fortunate. She was of such subtle <term>intellect</term><note>original: "engin". Refers to natural wit, ingenuity, or mental capacity.</note> that she could suddenly imitate anything she heard or saw said or done by another. She learned Greek and Latin letters, not as another woman might have done, but so vividly and with such good rhetoric that she composed letters and verses with such great excellence that those who read them said that doing so would have been a laudable thing even in an expert man well-taught in <term>scholarship</term><note>original: "clericature". The formal education, literacy, and Latin proficiency usually reserved for the clergy and male scholars.</note>. She was able, through her eloquence and elegance, to persuade and move those who listened to her toward games and entertainment, exciting them to laughter and to all manner of softness, delight, and <term>impudence</term><note>original: "procite". A term for boldness or a lack of modesty in social behavior.</note>. She knew how to elegantly sing, dance, and leap—things which, when used healthily and not viciously, are perhaps graces and gifts to be highly recommended in a woman. But she was filled with too great a boldness, stirring up the <term>lust</term><note>original: "luxure". In this period, it refers specifically to sexual desire and carnal excess.</note> of desire. Beyond and above the said vice of carnal desire, Sempronia was very much in love with gold and silver, and was profuse and profligate in spending and alienating wealth in whatever...

<meta>This page continues the biography of Sempronia, a Roman noblewoman involved in the Catiline conspiracy, focusing on her financial vices and her role in the plot against the Roman state.</meta>

...those vile and detestable vices. She sought money with such care, through very filthy gain and vile pursuit,
that in her greed and extravagance she held no
moderation or middle ground. And so that we may conclude with the last of her vices—
as I believe—while the fire of the mortal
pestilence of that most seditious man, Lucius Catiline <note>A Roman senator who attempted to overthrow the Republic in 63 BCE.</note>, was being 
kindled, and as through wicked counsels and the number of his associates and 
companions he was continually rising in greater and more extensive 
power toward the perpetual ruin of the Roman Republic <term>Roman Republic</term> <note>original: "chose publique rommaine," a literal translation of the Latin *res publica*</note>, 
this most vicious woman—in order to have greater 
freedom to continue her dishonest and lustful life, desiring 
that which even the most wicked and condemned men should 
abhor—joined herself with the said conspirators. And she 
opened to them the most secret parts of her house to hold their 
disloyal meetings. But by our Creator, who always 
opposes iniquity, and by the counsel of Marcus Tullius Cicero <note>The famous Roman orator and statesman who exposed the conspiracy.</note>, at that 
time the Roman Consul, the plans of the conspirators 
were discovered. And after Catiline had departed from Rome and 
retired to Fiesole <note>original: "Fesules," an ancient city in Tuscany where Catiline gathered his army.</note>, she—as I believe, seeing that she was 
deprived of Catiline’s company—consented to the 
destruction of the other conspirators. Therefore, although 
we might praise her wit <term>wit</term> <note>original: "engin," referring to her natural talent, intelligence, and eloquence.</note> and for that reason exalt her, 
nevertheless by necessity we must condemn her lasciviousness and 
filthy lusts; because she failed to maintain 
a moderate and temperate life, the stain of her villainy 
has remained with her forever.

**[Side text block]**
**E**ven among the number of women of the Cimbri <note>A Germanic or Celtic tribe that fought the Roman Republic in the late 2nd century BCE.</note>, who were conquered and overcome in battle by Gaius Marius, there was a very great and praiseworthy example of holy chastity and constant purpose. At the time when the deeds and affairs of the Romans were flourishing, the Teutons, the Cimbri, and several other barbarian nations...

**[Main text block]**
...and strange peoples, emerging from the northern regions <note>original: "septemtrion"</note> and living under the Arctic pole, formed a conspiracy to destroy the Roman name. And so that none among them should hope to rely on their own strength or turn in flight, they pulled and brought with them a very great number and train of wagons carrying their women, children, and their entire households. They did this specifically so that they might devastate all of Italy through a single assault and infestation, deciding to enter the land for a violent battle by three great directions and paths.

The astonished Romans sent Gaius Marius to meet them, in whom at that time all the hope of the Romans and the Republic <note>original: "chose publique," a translation of the Latin *Res Publica*.</note> was seen to reside. This Marius encountered the haughty and proud leaders of the Teutons; against them, he commenced a battle where, through long fighting, the fortune of both sides remained equal for a long time. At last, after much blood was spilled, the Teutons turned their backs and fled. From there, Marius went with his men against the Cimbri. And just as he had defeated the Teutons near the Waters of Sextius <note>The Battle of Aquae Sextiae (102 BCE), fought near modern-day Aix-en-Provence.</note>, he likewise defeated...

the said Cimbri <note>An ancient Germanic or Celtic tribe that migrated through Europe and threatened the Roman Republic</note> through a double battle in the Raudian Fields <note>The Battle of Vercellae, fought on the Raudian Plain in northern Italy, where the Romans decisively defeated the Cimbri</note> where
there was a very great slaughter of men. The defeats
becoming known to their wives, they did not follow

<meta>This page continues the biographies of famous women from antiquity, focusing here on Julia, the daughter of Julius Caesar.</meta>

**J**ulia, in regards to her lineage and marriage, was by fortune one of the most noble women in the world. She was the daughter of Julius Caesar and of Cornelia, his wife, who was the daughter of Cinna, a man who had four times held the office of <term>consul</term> <note>The highest elected political office in the Roman Republic; two were elected annually.</note>. This Julius Caesar traced his paternal descent from Aeneas, the most noble leader of the Trojans, through a long succession of kings and noblemen who followed him. Her maternal lineage proceeded from Ancus Marcius, formerly King of the Romans.

Furthermore, Julius Caesar earned a most noble reputation for his glory in battles, his triumphs, and his perpetual dictatorship. This Julia was married to the Great Pompey <note>Gnaeus Pompeius Magnus (106–48 BCE), a leading Roman general and statesman.</note>, who was at that time a man of the highest nobility among the Romans. He won the love and favor of the Roman people and the kings of the entire world by conquering them, deposing them from their kingdoms, and then restoring them anew; he subjugated nations and wiped out the pirates of the sea. 

He exerted himself across all lands and seas, and he loved this noble lady so dearly and passionately—even though she was young and he was already quite old—that she met her death long before her time because of this love. For while Pompey was performing a sacrifice at the <term>comitia</term> <note>The legal assemblies where Romans voted on laws or elected officials; here specifically refers to the elections of "aediles" or public works officers.</note> and aedile gatherings, his robe was heavily sprayed and splattered with blood coming from the wound and incision of the sacrificial beast, which was struggling violently. He stripped off that robe and sent it back to his house so that another might be brought to him.

<summary>This page introduces Julia, daughter of Julius Caesar and wife of Pompey the Great, detailing her illustrious ancestry and the beginning of the tragic incident involving a blood-stained robe.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Sulpicia (the daughter of Julia and wife of Lentulus Cruscellio), a Roman noblewoman famous for her devotion. It likely comes from a Middle French translation of Giovanni Boccaccio’s *De Mulieribus Claris* (Concerning Famous Women).</meta>

...exiled and poor. This Sulpicia, having certain knowledge of this matter, determined in her heart to wish to suffer all pains and labors with her husband. And she considered that it is not appropriate for women to want to share joy, honors, and prosperous fortune with their husbands, yet be unwilling to follow them in their miseries and poverty, should such a case arise. 

[¶] <note>original: "Summa quippe" (Indeed, the highest [virtue])</note> [¶] 

Certainly, she could not easily find a way to follow her husband. For Julia, her mother, guarded her with supreme diligence to ensure she did not follow him. But what guard can be so strong that true love cannot deceive and trick it? For this reason, Sulpicia chose and watched for an opportune time to leave; and having deceived her mother and her guards, she took the clothing of a servant girl and, accompanied by two valets and as many chambermaids, left her native country and her gods <note>A reference to the Roman household gods or the pagan religion of her ancestors.</note> and followed her husband into that exile. 

Even though by their law—which did not forbid it at all—she could have abandoned her unhappy husband and taken another by celebrating a new wedding, this noble lady did not hesitate at all to follow in the footsteps of her husband through various and perilous flights across the mountains in Italy, across rivers and seas. And she searched for him through regions unknown to her until she found him and joined him. She considered it a more honorable thing to thus follow her husband through a fortune so cast down, through a thousand perils and dangers to her life, than to live in her own country in rest and delights while her said husband was exiled and banished in poverty and toil. 

Certainly, such a judgment came from a noble mind and a very wise man, rather than from a woman. <note>The author employs a common historical trope, implying that Sulpicia’s extraordinary courage was a "masculine" virtue surpassing the expected nature of women at the time.</note> One cannot always be resplendent and adorned with gold and precious stones. One must not always be occupied with adorning oneself nor withdraw from labor. 

[¶] 

The great heat of the sun is not always present, nor are the rains of winter. One cannot and should not always be in one's chamber nor in one's prepared bed. And also, one should not always spare oneself and remain at rest; rather, women must—when fortunes so dictate—submit themselves to enduring pains and labors, suffer poverty, and with strong courage bear all perils and dangers with their [husbands]...

<margin>o iii</margin> <note>This is a "signature mark," used by early printers to ensure the pages were bound in the correct sequence.</note>

The text praises her loyalty as a "masculine" virtue and argues that true partnership requires sharing both prosperity and ruin.</summary>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of the Judean royal court during the transition from the Hasmonean dynasty to the reign of Herod the Great, focusing on the children of Alexandra (the daughter of Hyrcanus II).</meta>

Their father Aristobulus <note>Historically, this refers to the lineage of the Hasmoneans. Though the text mentions the father, the "she" following refers to Alexandra, the mother of Mariamne and Aristobulus III.</note> coveted and sought with supreme diligence from King Herod, husband of Mariamne, to have the High Priesthood of the Jews granted [to his son] through the persuasion of a friend of his named Gellius. As it is said, she caused an excellent painter to paint on panels the likenesses of her two children, Aristobulus and Mariamne; and these panels, thus painted, she sent to the aforementioned Antony <note>Mark Antony, the Roman triumvir.</note>, then being in Egypt, a man of most extreme lust, so that he might exercise his lust upon these children of hers.

And by that path, she hoped to lead him so that through his means her will and desire might be accomplished. When Antony saw these painted likenesses, he at first remained for a long time in admiration; then he said that as for the form and beauty of these two, they were the children of God. And he firmly affirmed that in no place had he ever seen any more beautiful, nor any similar to them in beauty.

But to return to Mariamne: if she was ennobled by unheard-of beauty, she was even more resplendent through her great and strong spirit. When she reached the age of marriage, she was married to Herod, son of Antipater, King of the Jews; and in her very great misfortune, she was supremely loved by him because of her beauty. This Herod greatly boasted of being the sole possessor in all the world of divine beauty. And he fell into great care and anxiety of heart as to how he could ensure that no one would be his equal in this, fearing that Mariamne might survive him and remarry another. 

To avoid this—as he first had to go to Egypt to see Antony, who had summoned him to explain the causes of the death of Aristobulus (Mariamne’s brother, whom Herod had caused to be killed), and later, after the death of the said Antony, he had to go to Octavian <note>The future Emperor Augustus.</note> to excuse himself, if he could, for having given aid to the friends of Antony against him—he left his mother and friends, whom he commanded that if anything happened to him that led to his death, they should have Mariamne killed immediately. 

**O** foolish and most ridiculous madness of this king!—who in his other deeds and business was very wise—to [begrudge] others the convenience, profit, utility, and pleasure... <note>The text breaks off here; "en deratiõ" suggests a lack of reason or a deprivation regarding the beauty of his wife.</note>

<summary>This page details Alexandra's plot to use her children's beauty to influence Mark Antony and describes King Herod’s obsessive, lethal jealousy regarding his wife, Mariamne.</summary>

...and gave himself pain and vexation before the event even happened.

¶ And even though this thing had been done secretly, Mariamne found out about it over time. And when already <note>original: "Et cū iam," a Latin fragment likely used as a transition from a Latin source text.</note> ¶ And because Mariamne had already conceived a terrible hatred against her husband Herod because of the most unworthy death of her brother Aristobulus <note>Aristobulus III was the last high priest of the Hasmonean line; Herod had him drowned in a swimming pool.</note>, and because she believed she was only loved by him for the use of her beauty, she accumulated one anger upon another within herself. She bore it very bitterly that he had thus condemned her life <note>referring to Herod's secret orders to have her killed if he died, so she would not belong to another</note>, even though she had conceived with him Alexander and Aristobulus, two sons of great beauty. And the said lady could not moderate the anger and hatred she had conceived; but in this fury she so impetuously threw herself that she refused to sleep with her husband who loved her so much. And she despised and refused him, bringing back into her mind all the pride <note>original: "iudoles," likely referring to the mental images or tokens of her ancestry</note> of her ancient and royal lineage with a haughty manner and bearing, and strove to lower and trample his power. Not fearing to often and openly say to her husband Herod that he was a foreigner, not a Jew nor a man of royal lineage, but ignoble and an Idumaean <note>Herod’s family were Edomite converts to Judaism, which his rivals used to claim he was not a "true" Jew or a legitimate king.</note>, unworthy of having a wife of royal lineage, and that he was fierce and proud, disloyal and evil, and a most cruel beast. 

Herod suffered these things with great difficulty, yet he did not dare, because of the love he had for her, to attempt any bad thing against her. But as time passed and things went from bad to worse—as pleased some—Cypros <note>original: "cyprienne"</note>, the mother of Herod, and Salome his sister, to whom Mariamne was very troublesome, bribed their cupbearer so that he would accuse Mariamne before Herod of having often begged him to present him a drink she had prepared <note>a "beurage," implying a poison or a love potion</note>. Since Herod believed this thing, already irritated and inflamed by agonizing fury, with a very long speech he made a complaint to their friends. By the persuasion of these friends, and even that of Alexandra, the mother of Mariamne—who believed that by this she might earn his favor <note>Alexandra turned against her own daughter to avoid being executed herself as a co-conspirator</note> because he had accused her of seeking the destruction and ruin of his royal majesty—she was condemned to have her head cut off.

¶ These things so excited and moved...

It highlights the ethnic and class tensions regarding Herod’s Idumaean heritage versus Mariamne’s royal Hasmonean blood.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the account of Mariamne, the wife of Herod the Great, and begins the biography of Cleopatra VII of Egypt. The narrative shifts from the moral examples of Judean royalty to the famous and controversial life of the last Pharaoh.</meta>

...the very noble courage of her <note>This refers to Mariamne, the wife of King Herod, whose execution was described on the previous page.</note> as she listened to the cries, scoldings, and reproaches of her mother. Even as her own death was vilified and made public, she faced it with a steady gaze and a full heart, without shedding any tears, while looking upon the others who were weeping. And as if in a joyous triumph—not only self-assured, but with a cheerful face and look—she went to the death she desired to receive at the hands of the executioner, without making any prayers for the safety of her life. This constant and firm certainty of courage did not only bring sadness to the envy of that unrestrained and cruel king <note>King Herod the Great</note>, but it also added more centuries to her fame than Herod could have granted her in months of mortal life, had he been moved by her tears and prayers.

## The 89th treatise on Cleopatra, Queen of Egypt
And begins in the text: <note>original: "Cleopatra egiptia feta &c."</note> *Cleopatra of Egypt, daughter [of...] etc.*

[Illustration: A king wearing a crown and holding a sword stands in a room, gesturing toward a woman in royal attire who stands before a window.]

**C**leopatra was an Egyptian woman, a legend and a mockery to the whole world. And although she took her birth and lineage successively through several kings—from King Ptolemy the Macedonian, and from his son Lagus <note>Lagus was the founder of the Ptolemaic dynasty after the death of Alexander the Great.</note>, and from Ptolemy Dionysus—or as it pleases some, she was the daughter of King Auletes <note>The transcription reads 'mineus', likely a scribal error for 'Auletes', the nickname of Ptolemy XII.</note>: nevertheless, she managed to rule the kingdom through craftiness and trickery. And because we begin with her reign and her domination, this Dionysus or Auletes, who was much loved by the Roman people, passed away during the time of the first consulate of Julius Caesar.

And at the hour of his death, he ordained in signed tablets by his last will and testament that the eldest of his sons, named Lysanias <note>This likely refers to Ptolemy XIII, though the manuscript uses the name Lysanias</note>, should take as his wife his sister Cleopatra, who was also the eldest of the daughters of the said Dionysus <note>Ptolemy XII was known as "Auletes" but also took the title "New Dionysus," which the French text renders as "Denis"</note>, their father. And he decreed that together, after his death, they should reign. 

This was done accordingly. But afterward, Cleopatra—burning with desire and greedy to rule the kingdom of Egypt alone—caused her brother and husband to be killed by poison. For the Egyptians exclude only mothers and daughters from their marriages <note>The author is explaining to a medieval audience that while sibling marriage was a scandal in Europe, Egyptian royal custom permitted it, forbidding only direct ancestors</note>.

wife Octavia, sister of Octavian Caesar, and with great affection joined himself by marriage to this same Cleopatra. <note>original: "Et dicit arabicas" — And he speaks of Arabian things.</note>

And so that I may omit recounting the aromatic ointments, the fragrant fumes from the region of Sheba, and the delicious wines and drinks they used—as this gluttonous man continuously fattened himself on magnificent meats and meals—he spoke to Cleopatra. Wishing to exalt and increase his banquets, he asked her what more magnificent thing he could add to his daily meals. To which the luxurious woman replied that, if she wished, she could consume at each meal a hundred times <term>two marks and a half of gold</term> <note>A "mark" was a medieval unit of weight for gold or silver, roughly 8 ounces. Cleopatra is claiming she can eat the value of over 40 pounds of gold in one sitting.</note>. Antony, knowing well that this could not be done, yet eager to see Cleopatra's undertaking, made a wager with her. And for this, they took Lucius Plancus as judge.

The following day, since Cleopatra did not exceed the usual dishes and meats in any way, Antony, mockingly, asked her for the wager he had won. She commanded the second course to be set and placed, upon which nothing was put but a single vessel full of vinegar. She then quickly pulled from one of her ears a pearl <note>The text says "pierre" (stone), but historical accounts specify it was one of the world's largest pearls.</note> of very rich and marvelous price, which she wore as an ornament according to the custom of the East, and threw it into the vinegar, where it was immediately dissolved; then she drank it.

And as she was putting her hands to her other ear to take a stone of equal value and do as she had with the other, the said Lucius Plancus, coming from the temple, judged Antony to be defeated. And by this means, the second stone was saved for this victorious queen. This stone was later split and divided in two, and one of the parts was carried to Rome to the temple of the <term>Pantheon</term> <note>The famous Roman temple dedicated to all gods.</note>, where the said part was placed on the ear of the goddess Venus. There, for a long time afterward, it bore witness to those looking upon it of Cleopatra’s "half-supper."

And as the insatiable greed which this woman had to reign and dominate grew in her more and more, so that all things might be joined together and fulfilled in her possession, she requested of Antony—who was then staggering and moved by the wine he had drunk—that he give her the Empire of Rome.

This Tiberius, holding her by the arm, rebuked, scolded, and harassed her until she wept, so much so that he could not appease her or make her grief cease, which he himself bore with impatience.

And for this very reason—specifically because he could not exercise dominion over her—he had this innocent woman brought before the Senate and accused her of several crimes; by means of this accusation, he had her put in prison. The said lady, bearing indignantly what had been done to her by Tiberius Caesar, resolved to end her life to stop the mockery and arrogance of the proud prince. And because no other opportunity for death was granted to her except through self-imposed hunger, she began to abstain from all food <note>original: *viādes*, meaning victuals or food in general</note>. 

When this was reported to Tiberius, and he understood the end toward which the young woman was aiming—so that she might not escape his insults <note>original: *iiures*, likely *injures*</note> by such a sudden path—and seeing that threats and beatings were of no use in making her eat, he was driven to such rage and madness that he had food forced into her throat by power, so that she might have nourishment against her will. But the more she was tormented by insults, the more she persevered in her purpose; and in dying, she vanquished the insolence of the most vicious and criminal prince, demonstrating that although this prince could easily, as he wished, [command] many... <note>The text breaks off here mid-word.</note>

## The 92nd chapter treats of Paulina, a Roman woman. And it begins in the text: *Paulina, a Roman woman of a certain foolish simplicity.*

[Miniature: A bedroom scene where a woman is being led to a bed by a man in a golden robe, while two monks <note>Medieval illustrators often depicted ancient priests as Christian monks. These figures represent the corrupt priests of the cult of Isis.</note> observe from the side.]

**P**aulina was a Roman woman who, through a certain foolish simplicity of her own, achieved almost perpetual renown. For during the reign of Tiberius Caesar, just as she was considered noble for the beauty of her mouth, her face, and the grace of her body above all other matrons <term>matron: a respected, high-ranking married woman in Roman society</term>: she likewise seemed to be the mirror and the very splendor <note>original: "speciosite"</note> of chastity.

His cult, along with that of Isis, was popular in Rome during this period.</note>, the god of the Egyptians, whom she worshipped and served with all her might. And certainly, as...

When the young man learned of this—with Love leading and instructing him—he imagined a fraud such as had never been heard of before. He considered that the priests of this god Anubis could help him, and he drew them to do his will through the very great gifts he gave them. For he brought in the oldest and most venerable among them, who told Paulina—when she had come to the temple according to her custom—that the god Anubis had appeared to him at night and had commanded him to tell her that he took great delight in her devotion toward him, and that he desired to speak with her and sleep with her in his temple. When Paulina heard this, believing it came about because of her own <term>holiness|Original: "sainctete," implying her religious merit and purity</term>, she began to greatly pride herself on the words she had heard. She placed as much faith in this command as if she had heard it with her own ears from the god Anubis himself; and she recounted it entirely to her husband, who—being even more foolish than his wife—granted her permission to go sleep in the temple. 

A noble bed, fit for a god, was then prepared in that temple without anyone knowing of it except her and the priests. When the shadows and darkness of night arrived, Paulina entered the temple, and all her attendants departed from it. After she had performed her prayers and sacrifices while waiting for the god, she got into her bed. While she was already asleep, Mundus was brought into the temple by the priests, covered and dressed in the garments of the god Anubis. He came to the bed and kissed Paulina, whom he loved so much, and woke her; upon waking, she was suddenly terrified. ### The 94th chapter treats Agrippina, mother of Nero. And begins in the text: <note>original: "Agrippina neronis cesaris mater"</note> Agrippina, the mother of Nero Caesar.

[Miniature: A woman in a blue dress sits in a golden carriage drawn by two horses. A man in a yellow tunic and red hose walks beside the horses, holding a whip. In the background is a castle or city gate.]

**A**grippina, mother of Nero Caesar, was illustrious no less for her lineage and her imperial bloodline, and for the monstrosity of her son, than for her own noble deeds. For she was the daughter of Germanicus Caesar <note>A beloved Roman general and heir to the empire.</note>, a youth of very good and praiseworthy character, and of the aforementioned Agrippina [the Elder]; and she was the sister of Julia, Agrippina, and the prince Gaius Caligula. She married Gaius Domitius of the family of the Ahenobarbi <note>Literally "Bronze-beards."</note>, a very wicked and proud man, by whom she conceived and gave birth to Nero, the most cruel beast in all the world, who emerged from his mother’s womb feet first <note>In antiquity, a breech birth was considered a terrible omen, signaling a life of hardship or a cruel nature.</note>.

Having obtained these things, and hoping that the beast Claudius had fallen and tumbled into her snares, Agrippina was not so much displeased that Claudius was continually drunk and glutted by his constant eating and banquets. This death was concealed and not publicly announced by the said Agrippina until Britannicus, with the help of her friends, had been set aside as being too young, and Nero was elected to the honor of the Empire. 

This deed done by Agrippina was so pleasing to Nero that he immediately preferred and placed his mother before all others in all the business and affairs of the Empire, both public and private. It reached such a point that Nero was seen to have only the title and name of Emperor, while his mother held the actual conduct and government of it. <meta>This page begins Chapter 94 of a Middle French translation of Giovanni Boccaccio's *De Mulieribus Claris* (Concerning Famous Women). It introduces the story of Epicaris, a woman of low social standing who became a key figure in the Pisonian conspiracy against the Emperor Nero.</meta>

### The 94th chapter treats of Epicaris, a freedwoman. And begins in the text *Epicaris extera etc.* <note>original Latin: "Epicaris extera etc." This is the opening line of the original Latin source text by Boccaccio.</note>

->[Miniature: A woman, Epicaris, is depicted hanging by the neck from a doorway. A small wooden stool lies overturned on the tiled floor beneath her. The scene is set within a room with two open doors.]<-

**E**picaris is more commonly believed to have been a foreign woman rather than a Roman, and she was not resplendent by any nobility of her ancestry. Instead, being born of a father who had been a slave before being set free, she was called a <term>libertine</term> <note>In this context, "libertine" refers to her legal status as a *libertina*, the daughter of a freed slave, rather than her moral character.</note>. And what is a more shameful thing, she did not take delight in any good or virtuous morals. Nevertheless, toward the end of her life, she demonstrated a different kind of heart through a manly strength of spirit. 

At that time, while the proud and detestable crimes, vices, luxuries, and other wickednesses of Nero the Roman Emperor were increasing for all Romans and Italians, it happened that the leader Lucius Piso, then a consul, several of the senators, and other citizens conspired to bring about the death of this Nero. And as they tried through various parleys and discussions to bring the undertaking to effect, the entire plan and the names of the conspirators came—I know not by what means—to the knowledge of this Epicaris. 

She, seeing in her judgment that this work was being carried out with too much delay, and being grieved and annoyed by this, departed from Rome and went to stay in the Campania of Apulia. And as she happened by chance to go to live in Puteoli <note>A port city near modern-day Naples.</note>, so that she would not let the time pass in vain, she became acquainted and struck a deal with Volusius Proculus, a <term>chiliarch</term> <note>A commander of a thousand men; the original French 'chinolarce' is a corruption of this Greek military title.</note>.

<summary>This page introduces Epicaris, a woman of humble origins and questionable character who nonetheless played a pivotal role in the conspiracy to assassinate the Emperor Nero. It describes how she learned of the plot and, frustrated by the conspirators' hesitation, took matters into her own hands by seeking allies in Puteoli.</summary>

<keywords>Epicaris, Nero, Pisonian conspiracy, Roman history, freedwoman, Lucius Piso, Puteoli, Volusius Proculus</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Pisonian conspiracy against the Emperor Nero, focusing on the role of the freedwoman Epicharis.</meta>

...the prefect of the Roman fleet <note>This refers to Volusius Proculus, an admiral who had been a primary agent in the murder of Nero's mother, Agrippina</note> who had formerly killed Agrippina, believing that he would be a great help in leading this conspiracy, and intending with all her power to enlist him and make him a companion of the conspirators. 

But it happened very differently than Epicharis <note>A freedwoman who, despite her status, proved more loyal to the cause than many Roman senators</note> intended. For Volusius, wanting to test if he could win back the prince’s favor through his services, as soon as he could reach Nero, revealed to him everything Epicharis had said to him. 

However, the matter did not go as he expected. For the wise and cautious woman, while he remained in uncertainty, had not yet revealed any of the names of the conspirators. Therefore, when she was summoned by Nero and brought before him, neither entreaties, threats, nor promises could ever make her willing to name any of the conspirators. 

While she was being held in prison, and after the <term>conspiracy</term> <note>The Pisonian conspiracy of 65 AD, a major plot to overthrow Nero and replace him with the statesman Gaius Calpurnius Piso</note> was discovered by the conspirators themselves, she was again called back for interrogation. This was done so that through the unbearable pain she would suffer from the <term>torment</term> <note>A reference to judicial torture, which was legally permitted against slaves and freedpersons in ancient Rome to extract evidence</note> of the men—and because the others had already confessed and accused themselves—they might more easily draw from her the information they wanted to know.

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Pisonian conspiracy against the Emperor Nero, specifically contrasting the endurance of the woman Epicharis with the weakness of her male accomplices. It then transitions into the biography of Pompeia Paulina, the wife of the philosopher Seneca.</meta>

Regarding the men of that conspiracy—who, through their own confessions or by being identified by others besides Epicharis <note>A freedwoman and member of the Pisonian conspiracy. Despite being tortured until her limbs were broken, she refused to name her accomplices, eventually taking her own life to maintain silence.</note>—none, however much they were in the prime of robust youth, was able to endure for his own safety what that woman suffered to save others. What is more, they could not even bear to hear the names of the tortures. Instead, they immediately disclosed everything they knew about the conspiracy to those questioning them. Thus, not one of them spared himself or his friends, whereas this noble woman would have spared them all, except herself.

¶ I might believe that Nature had occasionally erred in such matters, as if she had placed the courage and thought into a woman’s breast that she had intended for a masculine body. But since God is the giver of such things, it is not permissible to believe that He was asleep at His work. Let us therefore consider women to be perfect in their design, for so we ought to believe; and if we do not fully believe it, His handiwork itself is the judge.

¶ And certainly, I believe that men should be ashamed that a frail and weak woman was so constant in enduring all pains, while they were defeated. For if men do not surpass women in character, they are rightfully considered effeminate <note>In this medieval context, "effeminate" is used as a term of reproach for men who lack the "manly" virtue of fortitude.</note> compared to them.

### Chapter 99 treats of Pompeia Paulina, wife of Lucius. And begins "Pompeia Paulina."

**P**ompeia Paulina was the noble wife of Lucius Annaeus Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – 65 AD), the famous Stoic philosopher, statesman, and former tutor to Emperor Nero.</note>, the tutor of Nero. Whether she was Roman or a foreigner, I do not recall having read. However, when I consider the nobility of her spirit, I prefer to believe she was Roman rather than from any other country. Although we may lack further details about her, we do not lack the example of her very sweet and kind love toward her husband.

¶ Many of the most honorable men of that time believed that through the cruelty of Nero, rather than by any crime committed by Seneca, this Seneca—a man old and very renowned—was marked with the stain of the Pisonian conspiracy <note>A major plot in 65 AD to assassinate the tyrant Nero. Seneca was accused of being part of the plot and was ordered to commit suicide.</note> (if indeed it should rightly be called a "stain" to take action against a ty... <note>The text cuts off mid-word as it reaches the end of the page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the downfall of the philosopher Seneca and the reaction of his wife, Paulina, during the reign of Emperor Nero. It follows a description of the conspiracies surrounding Nero's court.</meta>

[Miniature: A scene depicting the death of Seneca. Seneca is shown sitting in a bathtub, his veins being opened by a man in blue. His wife, Paulina, stands next to him, also having her veins opened. A centurion <note>A professional officer of the Roman army.</note> in armor and another man stand by as witnesses.]

...under the shadow of which, because of the ancient hatred that Nero by his nature held against all virtues, this path of violence was found by him to be used against Seneca. Although some say that by the impulse of Pompey and Tigillinus <note original: "tigillinus">Ofonius Tigillinus was the powerful prefect of the Praetorian Guard and a notorious advisor to Nero, often blamed for the Emperor's most cruel decisions.</note>—who was the sole advisor to the Emperor's cruelty—the matter was brought to such a point

that a centurion told and announced to Seneca that he should choose by what death he wished to die. And when Paulina saw her husband being led away to be executed and to die, she cast aside and put away the <term>blandishments</term> <note>Gentle, flattering, or affectionate words used to soothe.</note> with which she had been accustomed to comfort and exhort her husband during his life. And being moved and led by the impulse of her very chaste love, she prepared to receive death with him with great courage, and in that same manner which he had chosen, so that they, who had lived a virtuous life joined together, might thus joined together finish it. 

To give up her spirit together with her husband, she had her veins opened. However, by the command of Nero—who had no particular hatred against her, and who wished to somewhat suppress the infamy of his natural cruelty—he had her saved from death by his guards, despite her own wishes. But truly, her blood could not be so quickly stanched that this very good woman did not retain a permanent paleness as a witness to having tried to separate her body from life alongside her husband. While for a few years, through praiseworthy widowhood, she preserved the memory of her husband—as she could do in no other way except by bearing his name alone—she, the wife of Seneca, finally closed and finished her final day.

q iiii <note>This is a quire mark, a printer's or scribe's notation used to keep the pages of the book in the correct order during binding.</note>

<summary>This page recounts the attempted double suicide of the philosopher Seneca and his wife Paulina after Nero ordered Seneca's death. While Seneca died, Paulina was forcibly saved by Nero's guards to avoid further public scandal, living the rest of her life in pale, virtuous widowhood.</summary>
<keywords>Paulina, Seneca, Nero, Tigillinus, suicide, Roman history, virtue, widowhood, Stoicism</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
What other thing except the sweetness of love and the clear and pure sign and demonstration of piety <note>original: "pitie"; in this context, it refers to religious devotion or dutiful affection</note>, and the venerable sacrament of marriage, could have persuaded that very good woman to rather wish she could honorably die with her old husband than to preserve her life to enter into second nuptials <note>remarriage</note>, as many women often do, and not without great shame to themselves.

And certainly, to the very great dishonor of the chastity that matrons <note>respectable married women</note> ought to keep, many of them in these days—I do not speak of going to second or third weddings, which is common to almost all—but to sixth, seventh, or eighth weddings, if the case so happens. It is so familiar to them to carry into the chambers of their husbands the torches and brands of new weddings <note>the traditional lighting used in marriage processions</note> that it seems they have taken their husbands from harlots <note>original: "ribauldes"</note>, whose custom it is that by night they very often change their beds and rascals <note>original: "ribaulx"; referring to low-life companions or paramours</note> to go and sleep elsewhere with others. Nor do they renew or restart their marriages in any other way, nor enter them any more shamefully than if they were performing a most holy service to all virtue <note>original: "honnestete"</note>. 

And likewise, it is not certain enough whether one should say such women have come out of a stall in a brothel <note>original: "bourdeau"</note> or from the chamber of their husbands. Nor do I doubt that one should suspect whether he or she who enters into such weddings acts more vilely and dishonestly, or whether he or she who is introduced and enters into them acts more foolishly. 

Ah, miserable and wretched ones that we are! How corrupted are our morals in this day. The women of old, whose hearts <note>original: "couraige"; in Middle French, this often refers to one's inner spirit or moral inclination</note> were prompt and inclined toward all sanctity, judged it a disgraceful thing to re-enter—I do not say into a seventh—but even into a second marriage; and from then on, such women could not mingle or associate with honest matrons. 

But the women of today do quite otherwise. For by nourishing and continuing their foul, scurvy <note>original: "roigneuse"; literally "mangy," used here as a moral insult</note>, and most stinking lust, thinking themselves more beautiful and dear, and saying they are young, they believe they know more by virtue of their many and continual marriages...

<summary>This page contains a moralistic critique of women who remarry multiple times, contrasting the perceived "filth" of modern serial marriage with the "sanctity" of ancient women who remained loyal to a single husband even after death.</summary>
<keywords>marriage, remarriage, chastity, matrons, morality, Middle French literature, social critique</keywords>

### The 96th chapter treats Poppaea Sabina, wife of Nero Caesar. And it begins in the text: "Sabina Pompeia, a Roman woman," <note>original Latin: "Sabina pompeia romana & cetera"</note> and so on.

**Poppaea Sabina** was a Roman woman and the very noble daughter of a man named Titus <note>The manuscript writes "Tollus," referring to Titus Ollius, a man whose career was ruined by political scandal.</note> who was not a man of the highest nobility. However, she did not take her name from him, but rather from Pompeius Sabinus, her maternal grandfather, a noble man honored with triumphs and ennobled by the office of consul. Nor were other feminine gifts <term>douaires</term> <note>Literally "dowries," here meaning natural talents or physical assets.</note> lacking in her, if only she had possessed a noble heart. 

For she was of marvelous beauty and entirely like her mother, who in beauty exceeded all other Roman women of her time. And furthermore, she possessed sweet speech, a lovely voice, and a sound of praiseworthy sweetness; she had a noble and subtle wit <note>original: "engin"</note>, had she only applied it to honest arts and sciences. She had a constant habit of appearing simple and modest in public, while in private and in her secret life, she practiced dainty dissolution <note>A phrase suggesting a lifestyle of luxury, vanity, and loose morals.</note>, which is the common vice of women. And although she seldom showed herself in public, nonetheless she did not fail to possess great art and cunning. For as she...

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of Poppaea Sabina’s character and her calculated use of beauty, transitioning into her marriages to Rufrius Crispinus and Otho, and her eventual introduction to the Emperor Nero.</meta>

...understood that the multitude of people, and even the highest nobility, took delight and pleasure in the sight of her mouth, she never left her home without that part being covered and hidden by a veil. <note>original: "couurechief" — a head-covering or kerchief. Here, the author suggests she used it as a strategic fashion choice to increase her mystery.</note> This was not out of a wish to hide what she wanted people to covet, but rather so that she would not satiate the eyes of onlookers by showing it too freely; and furthermore, that which she had hidden with a veil or kerchief, she made them desire even more to see.

¶ And so that I do not dwell on all her habits—as she never spared her own reputation—she directed her lust and pleasure toward wherever the greatest profit appeared to be, without making any distinction or concern between married men, adulterers, or ruffians. This woman, though stained by such notoriety, was favored by a fortune quite pleasant and propitious to her. For as she had wealth and means in great abundance to support the glory of her birth and lineage, she first married Rufrius Crispinus, <note>A commander of the Praetorian Guard who was later executed by Nero.</note> a Roman knight. <term>Knight: translated from "cheuaucheur," referring to the "Equites," the social class of wealthy Romans just below the senatorial rank.</term>

And as she already had a son by him, she was led by the suggestion and urging of Otho—a young man who was heavily devoted to luxury and every pleasure, and held such great authority with Nero that he was the partner of his bed <note>An expression indicating Otho was one of Nero's most intimate inner circle of friends.</note>—this wicked and adulterous woman coupled with him carnally, and it was not long after that she became and was made his wife. 

¶ But truly, Otho—either because the heat of the love he felt for her made him less cautious, or because he could no longer endure the fickle and wicked nature of such a woman and for this reason tried to lead her into the lust of Nero, or perhaps by the destiny of Poppaea which was meant to be so—would often, upon rising from Nero’s table, say that he was returning to the one upon whom the sovereign gods had bestowed all nobility. That is to say, he praised her elegance of manners and divine beauty, in which consisted all mortal desires and the joys and pleasures of the blessed. By these words, Nero’s lusts were easily stirred, and the path was cleared—not by long prayers, requests, or entreaties, but...

<meta>This page is part of a series of biographies of notable women from antiquity, likely based on Giovanni Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris" (On Famous Women). It follows the accounts of Roman noblewomen and focuses on the civil wars of 69 AD.</meta>

[Miniature: A coastal city with a tall tower on the left. Several ships are in the harbor. Soldiers in armor are visible on the ships and on the shore. One ship has a large white sail.]

**T**riaria was not known for any particular brilliance of her lineage, except for the fact that she was the wife of Lucius Vitellius, brother of Aulus Vitellius, the Roman Emperor <note>Aulus Vitellius was one of four emperors who ruled in quick succession during the chaotic "Year of the Four Emperors" in 69 AD.</note>. Concerning her—either because of the fervent love she bore for her husband or because of the cruelty naturally rooted in her spirit—so great was her ferocity that, through behaviors contrary to the usual conduct of women, she was deemed worthy of being remembered.

¶ At the time when Vitellius Caesar and Vespasian were contending for the dignity of the Empire, it happened that several gladiators <term>gladiators: in this period of civil war, bands of gladiators were often pressed into service as irregular soldiers</term> had entered Terracina—a city of the Volsci <note>The Volsci were an ancient Italic people; Terracina is a coastal city south of Rome.</note>—which was under a Roman leader named Julian. Many sailors from the Roman fleet, which was then anchored in the sea near Mount Circe under the prefect Apollinaris, also sided with Vespasian. Because of Julian's folly and negligence, which was reported and guided by a slave, Lucius Vitellius entered that city by night. Treating the citizens like enemies as they were half-asleep, seizing their weapons, and stirring themselves, he fell upon them and committed a great slaughter. 

Triaria, who had followed her husband and entered the city with him by night, desiring victory for her husband, had girt herself with a sword and armor. She mingled with Vitellius’s knights, appearing now here, now there, in the midst of the darkness and gloom of the night, amid the discordant and varied clamor, cries, and other proclamations, throwing javelins, arrows, and other weaponry <note>original: "arnois" – referring to military equipment or armor</note> as she ran about.

Therefore, it clearly appears that this woman—under whichever master or teacher she may have been introduced—was above all else instructed in the <term>liberal arts</term> <note>The "liberal arts" were the traditional core of classical education, consisting of the Trivium (grammar, logic, rhetoric) and the Quadrivium (arithmetic, geometry, music, astronomy).</note>. <meta>This page appears to continue a biographical or moralizing account of a specific woman—likely the Roman poet Proba—praising her for choosing intellectual and spiritual study over traditional domestic chores or idle gossip.</meta>

...nor as this woman did in applying what she took from the sayings and writings of the heathens <note>This likely refers to Falconia Proba, a 4th-century poet who created a "Cento," a poem made entirely of lines by the pagan poet Virgil but rearranged to tell the story of the Bible.</note>. It would have been enough for her—if we properly consider the usual customs of women—to keep to the spindle, the needle, and the craft of weaving. She might have done so had she wished to spend her life in a foolish manner <note>original: "nicement", implying a lack of purpose or simple-mindedness</note> as many other women do. 

But because, through her continuous and diligent study of the holy scriptures, she cleansed and wiped away the stream of laziness from her mind <note>original: "engin", referring to her natural talent or intellect</note>, she escaped through the darkness of night into everlasting light.

**L**ikewise, would that it please God that those who follow and serve their pleasures and idleness might look with a brave heart upon this example. These are people to whom it seems a great and praiseworthy thing to sit upon a couch, passing unrecoverable time in frivolous fables and lies. From early morning until deep into the night, they occupy themselves either with harmful or vain words, spent in defaming others, or in pampering themselves <note>original: "mignotant"</note> and living in luxury. Truly, they would recognize how great a difference there is between seeking to gain renown through praiseworthy works, and burying one's name along with one's own corpse <note>original: "charoigne", literally meaning carrion or decaying flesh</note>.

<summary>This passage praises an educated woman for studying the scriptures and classical texts instead of settling for domestic labor or idle gossip, contrasting her lasting fame with the spiritual death of the lazy.</summary>

Through the vile and dishonorable actions of this Commodus Antoninus, her son, it was more easily believed that he was the son of the gladiator than the son of Antoninus. 

When these things became truly apparent, to the shame of Faustina, Antoninus was urged by his friends to put her to death, or at the very least—if he wished to act more humanely—to divorce and abandon her. But truly, as Antoninus was of a gentle nature, even though he bitterly bore the adultery and debauchery of his wife, he refused to follow the advice of his friends. Lest he should die in even greater dishonor, he preferred to endure these things. To his friends who urged him otherwise, he replied only that for divorced wives, it was necessary to return their dowries—intending by this to mean that he held his position <note>The Imperial throne</note> by virtue of his marriage to Faustina.

But these matters are best left aside. For it often happens that even through a very slight look, performed unwisely or without thought, even the most honorable people have been vilified. She, having been asked by the consul, agreed to go. And there, just as for the other senators, a very noble seat was honorably prepared for her; and according to the custom of the others, she gave her opinion on what was then to be done—an event which has never occurred in any living memory. But of this we think very little and take no account <note>The author, Boccaccio, expresses traditional medieval disdain for women holding political office, which was historically forbidden in the Roman Senate.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the biography of the Byzantine Empress Irene, beginning with a mention of her illness and the subsequent coup by Nicephorus, and transitions into the story of Gualdrada of Florence.</meta>

...by the oppression of illness.

**A**fter she had once again ruled for five years, she was besieged in the Eleutherian Palace by Nicephorus, who had rebelled against her. He had received the diadem of the empire from the hand of Tarasius <note>original: "acharsius"; likely referring to Tarasius, Patriarch of Constantinople</note>, the Patriarch of Constantinople, through the favor of Leo and Triphyllius the patricians, and Siccopeus the guardsman <term>guardsman: translated from 'satellaire', referring to a member of the imperial bodyguard or a satellite official</term> whom she had recently enriched from her own treasury. He acted so toward Irene that he gained access to her; she believed, through his flatteries, that he desired nothing from the empire—as he himself claimed—except for that palace alone. Once her request was granted and obtained by him, and his oath regarding these matters was given to her, she opened and showed him all her treasures. Having obtained these, the most wicked man—betraying his faith to her—sent her and caused her to be sent into exile on the island of Lesbos, where this most noble woman, having now become old, ended her life.

**O**thers, however, feel quite differently about her end, saying that as the mother and the son were in discord, each in turn depriving their companion of the empire, the Romans withdrew from their subjection and transferred their empire to Charlemagne <note>original: "charles le grant"</note>, then King of France. They attempted to restore and rejoin the empire into one—which was then divided in two—by joining Irene to him in marriage, to which she consented. **E**t as the patrician Aetius <note>original: "enticius"</note> was informed of these things, he immediately raised Nicephorus to the empire and, by siege, forced her to leave the empire and enter religion in a monastery, where she grew old and finally died.

### Famous and Noble Women

This woman, therefore, as she was in the temple once dedicated to **Mars** <note>The Florentines believed their Baptistery was originally a Roman temple dedicated to the god Mars.</note> and since then dedicated to the **true God** under the name of **Saint John the Baptist**, was celebrating the solemnity of a feast day one day along with several other matrons of **Florence**. It happened that **Otho the Fourth**, Emperor of the Romans, who by chance had then come to Florence to enjoy the feast and increase its grandeur by his presence, accompanied by a very great number of his princes and kin, entered that temple.

And as he looked out from the highest seat at the decoration of the temple, the citizens walking about, and the matrons and other women seated all around, it happened by chance that he cast his gaze upon this **Gualdrada**. After he had observed her form, beauty, bearing, and manner for a long time with great admiration—praising her virtue and the gravity of this young woman, for she was a maiden—he turned his words to one of the citizens named **Bellincione**.

<note>original: "bilicon" refers to Bellincion Berti, a nobleman and father of Gualdrada.</note>

This man, venerable in age and nobility and at that time highly renowned in deeds of arms and chivalry, was standing there. The Emperor said to him:

¶ "I pray you, who is that maiden sitting across from us, who in our judgment surpasses all others in virtue and physical beauty?"

To which **Bellincione**, smiling and in a joyful and courtly manner, replied:

¶ "Most noble and peaceful prince, whoever she may be, she is such that whenever you wish, she will kiss you if I command her."

Having heard this response, the maiden was immediately indignant, suffering it with ill patience for... <note>Gualdrada’s famous refusal follows on the next page, where she asserts that no man shall kiss her except her future husband.</note>

--- Page 86 ---
By the favor of Him <note>Referring to God</note> whose most holy name you have strived to deceive and mock through your false and wicked perjury, I have rightfully caused your lie to recoil upon you—a matter concerning you and your marriage that is of great significance to me. You believed, I think, while you were still a prisoner and locked away, that I—forgetful and unmindful of my own social standing—presumptuously desired and longed for a royal marriage, and that I was set ablaze by your beauty through womanly lust. You thought that once you were delivered and set at liberty by my own wealth, which I paid out for you, you could clear yourself with a single denial, laughing and mocking, crushing and trampling me down; and that once you were restored to your former honors, you could save yourself for a more illustrious marriage.

And in so far as it lay with you, you have done so basely. Truly, He who looks down from on high upon humble things and never abandons those who hope in Him, knowing the purity of my thoughts, has brought it about through my slight effort that I might shatter your frauds, uncover your ingratitude, and demonstrate your disloyalty. Nor did I do this thing for my own profit or advantage, but only out of detestation for your wickedness.

For your brothers can see, and all others can see as well, what matters—and in what manner—are committed to your faith <note>original: "foy"; here meaning his word or oath of honor</note>, and what you and your friends can hope for, and your enemies fear. I have lost my gold, and you your reputation; I have lost my hope, and you the favor of the king and of your friends. The matrons of Sicily marvel at my magnificence <note>term: magnificence — in this context, it refers to the Aristotelian virtue of "great-souled" generosity or noble spending for a high purpose</note> and exalt me with great praise; but to all those who know you—and even to those who do not—you have become a spectacle of detestable mockery and ignominious laughter.

In this, however, I was for some time deceived. For I foolishly believed that for a wicked and proud piece of earth <note>original: "fiel de terre"; a derogatory expression for a base or worthless person</note>, I had removed and delivered from bonds a most noble and royal youth; whereas I see now that I have delivered a liar, a teller of tall tales, a disloyal man, a scoundrel <note>original: "lecherre"; a villain or person of low morals</note>, and a most cruel and wild beast.

**N**e do not think so much of yourself as to believe that you alone led me to do what I have done for you. For it was the memory of the past favors of your fa... <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "father"</note>
