<meta>This page continues a speech of rebuke, likely from a literary or historical narrative, where a woman named Cannosse confronts a prince for his ingratitude after she ransomed him from captivity.</meta>

...and father toward me. If your father was King Frederick of holy memory <note>original: "ſaincte recordation," a formulaic expression used to refer to a deceased person of high status, similar to "of blessed memory."</note>, I can hardly believe that such an excellent and renowned prince could have fathered such a dishonest son.

¶ You have deemed it an unworthy thing for a widow, not of royal blood, to have married a man of that blood—a robust youth, resplendent in form and beauty.

Which thing I will fully confess. But truly, I wish—if you could do so by right—that you would answer me this: when, through my gift and merit, I sought to make you mine, and when I paid a very great sum of my gold for your liberty, where then was your royal splendor? Where was your strength and vigor, then untested? Where was the honor of your shining face? The obscurity of the dark cave where you lay covered all these things. The filth of the rust from the chains, the paleness caused by the dim light, and the stench of that horrible dungeon.

¶ Because of these things, you, sick and powerless, were wasting away and growing thin, filthy and stinking, and forgotten by all men. These conditions had suppressed those "dowries" <note>Here referring to his natural virtues, beauty, and status</note> which you now, haughty and proud, so highly exalt.

¶ Back then, you told me that I was worthy not just of a royal youth, but of a celestial god. ¶ O, most wicked man, how quickly and easily—after you saw the sky of your own country beyond all your hopes—you changed your mind! You do not remember, now that you have come into your freedom, that I am Cannosse, who alone remembered you. I alone had pity and mercy for your misfortune, and I alone spent my own substance for your safety and redemption.

I am Cannosse, who took you from the hands of the arch-enemy <term>captal</term> <note>A title of nobility (Lord) used in Gascony, here likely referring to a specific high-ranking enemy leader</note> of your leaders and ancestors, and from your king; I took you from the chains of the dungeon of a miserable life by my own money. I restored you to your country. I brought you back to the royal house and palace and into your former light; and from a wretched and filthy thing, I made of you a robust and beautiful royal youth.

¶ What is it that I recall and bring back to your mind? It is that which you ought to remember and which you cannot deny. ¶ You, truly, in exchange for such memorable benefits, have returned me these present "thanks"—that you have dared...

<meta>This page continues a speech from the previous page, where a woman is confronting a man named Roland regarding his betrayal of their marriage bond.</meta>

...to deny our marriage, approved by holy and honest witnesses, and by contracts and signed letters; and to shamefully despise, ignore, and vilify her who ransomed you, if you could, through base suspicion.

¶ But, man of a corrupt mind, you were ashamed to have as a wife a widow born and begotten of a man of the knightly order. <note>original: "ordre des cheuaucheurs," referring to the class of horsemen or knights, the lower nobility.</note>

¶ Oh, how much better and more holy it would have been for you to be ashamed of having broken the faith you pledged and given, and of having thus held in contempt the holy and terrible name of God, and through your wretched ingratitude to have shown how full of vices you are. I confess that I am not a woman of royal blood.

¶ But since from my childhood, and barely out of my cradle, <note>original: "bers," meaning cradle.</note> I have associated with royal virgins, noblewomen, and daughters-in-law, <note>original: "fillaſtres," which in Middle French could mean step-daughters or daughters-in-law.</note> it is no wonder if I have adopted their manners and spirit, which should be enough to attain royal nobility.

¶ But why should I say so many things? I will be gracious and favorable toward you in this matter, where you, with all your might, have been difficult toward me.

¶ You denied that you were mine, even though you were; and though you are mine, now that I have conquered you, I grant you of my own will that you belong to me no longer.

¶ Let the royal splendor be yours—though it is defiled by your infidelity—and let the strength of youth and your fleeting beauty <note>original: "caducque," meaning transitory or frail.</note> be yours; as for me, from now on, content with my widowhood, I will restrain myself so that the wealth and fortunes God has lent me may be left to heirs more honorable than those who would be fathered by you. Go then, miserable youth! <note>original: "iouvencel," a young man.</note>

¶ For you have made yourself unworthy of having me. Learn at your own expense by what arts and deceits you may trick <note>original: "truſſer," to pack off, deceive, or mock.</note> and mock other women; for me, it is enough to have been deceived by you once, and so I desire never to be with you again. And truly I believe and judge that keeping and maintaining a heavenly life and holy widowhood is much to be preferred over your embraces and kisses.

¶ And having said these things, she removed herself from the sight of this Roland; from that moment on, no matter what prayers or warnings were given, she could not be turned from her praiseworthy resolution. And Roland, confounded

¶ and repenting too late...

Abandoned to his folly and disregarded by all, with a downcast gaze, fleeing the sight not only of his brothers but of all the people, he went away into a miserable fortune, not daring by right to reclaim what he had by right denied. And the King and all the nobles, greatly marvelling at the noble courage of that woman, exalted her with marvelous praises, uncertain which was more to be commended: whether that, contrary to feminine scarcity <note>original: "escharcite feminine" — a reference to the medieval trope that women were naturally more frugal or had fewer resources</note>, she had ransomed the youth with such a great sum of money; or whether, having ransomed him and proved him ungrateful and without any merit, she, as a courageous woman, had cast him off and refused him.

### Chapter 157 treats Joan, Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily. 
### And begins in the text: Joan of Jerusalem and Sicily <note>original: "Johana ihslm & sicillie"</note>

Joan, Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily, beyond all other women of our time in birth, in power, and in character, was a noble woman. This Joan, then, was the daughter of the very noble and peaceable prince Charles, the noble Duke of Calabria, firstborn of the late Robert of noble memory, King of Jerusalem and Sicily, and of Mary, sister of Philip, King of France. If we wish to search out her parents and ancestors to the very end, we would not cease until, climbing through innumerable kings, we had come to Dardanus <note>Dardanus was the legendary founder of Troy, from whom many European royal houses claimed descent to establish ancient legitimacy</note>, the first author and founder of the castle of...

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Queen Joanna I of Naples (reigned 1343–1382), focusing on her illustrious lineage and the vast geographic extent of her inherited territories.</meta>

...Amphion <note>The text likely refers to the mythological King of Thebes, though the sentence begins mid-word.</note> her father, of whom the ancients say was Jupiter. 

¶ From such an ancient and so noble a lineage have come noble princes from all sides, so many that there is no Christian king who is not related to her by blood or marriage; thus, no other woman in the time of our fathers or our own has appeared more noble. This same woman, while still a small child, lost her father Charles to an untimely death while he was still a young man. Because her grandfather Robert was left with no heir of the better sex <note>original: "meilleur sexe"; a reference to the medieval legal preference for male heirs (primogeniture)</note>, it happened by his command that upon his death she was made heir and succeeded to the kingdom.

And her great heritage did not fall to her in some distant land like Libya <note>original: "laida," a variant of Lybia</note> which sits upon the burning belt of the sun, nor beyond the Sarmatians <note>Ancient nomadic peoples of the Eurasian steppe</note> under the rigor of the cold of the "polar-antarctic" <note>A poetic reference to the extreme North</note> ice. 

¶ But rather her lands lie between the Adriatic and Tyrrhenian seas, stretching from the borders of Umbria and Picenum and the ancient land of the Volsci <note>Ancient Italic tribes</note> down to the Sea of Sicily, under a mild and temperate sky. The inhabitants within these boundaries and limits obey her empire and command. ¶ Such as the Campanians, the ancient Lucanians, the Bruttians, and those of Salento. ¶ The Daunians, Vestini, and Slavonians, the Paeligni, the Marsi, and several others—not to mention the greater titles such as the Kingdom of Jerusalem, the island of Sicily, and territories in Cisalpine Gaul <note>Northern Italy</note>. The territory of Piedmont is also hers, though it is currently occupied by some who usurp it from her. 

¶ Also those who inhabit the province of Septimania <note>A region in southern France</note> between Narbonnese Gaul, the Rhone, and the Alps, and the County of Forcalquier likewise obey her commands and confess her to be their queen and lady. 

¶ Oh, how many noble cities are there in these regions! How many other noble towns and castles! How many harbors and seaports and refuges for mariners! How many ships, how many lakes, how many holy and medicinal springs! How many woods and forests, and how many retreats and delightful places and rich, fertile fields! 

¶ Likewise, how many populous nations, and how many great princes and lords besides! ¶ How great also are the riches there, and the abundance of all things belonging to life. Certainly...

<meta>This page continues the praise of a female sovereign, likely a queen or high-ranking noblewoman, focusing on her ability to establish law and order in a territory previously plagued by bandits and defiant lords. It transitions from her military and legal successes to a description of her personal character and intellect.</meta>

It would be no small thing to explain.

**A**nd since this involves a very great lordship, and one that has not been accustomed to being possessed by women, it brings her no less clarity than wonder if we look at it closely. And what is a much greater marvel is her courage; it is sufficient that she has always kept and still keeps the resplendent character <term>character: the original French "indole" refers to an innate disposition or inherited noble nature</term> of her ancestors. 

And after that region was ennobled by the royal diadem <term>diadem: a crown or jeweled headband worn as a symbol of sovereignty</term>, by rising up and showing power and virtue, she purged the land so thoroughly that she cleared not only the cities and domestic and private places, but also the high mountains and the trackless valleys, the forests and the caverns of wild beasts. From these places, she drove out and put to flight the hands of thieves and murderers—to all of whom she was terrifying—or she hemmed them in within the high mountain fortresses. These criminals, by a company of men-at-arms under a noble duke, she caused to be besieged, commanding him that the siege <note>original: "obsidion"</note> should never cease or be lifted until, the fortresses being captured, he had put those criminal men to death by torment. This is something which none of the previous kings either wished to do or were able to do. 

And she has so ordered the lands she possesses that not only the poor, but the rich as well, singing by day and by night, can safely find their way wherever they wish. It is a no less healthy thing that she has restrained and curbed the great lords with such great moderation and temperance <note>original: "attrempance", a cardinal virtue involving self-control</note>, and led their dissolute morals back to a better life, so that ancient pride is removed and completely cast down. Those who formerly despised her kings and made no account of them today tremble at the face of this wrathful woman.

**S**he is also a woman so well-advised and so clear-sighted that you could sooner deceive her by fraud than by wit <note>original: "engin", referring to cleverness or ingenuity</note> and understanding.

**S**he is magnificent and of great excellence, holding more to the manner of a king than of a woman in this regard.

**S**he is likewise agreeable and of great grac... <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "grace" or "gradu" (rank/dignity)</note>

...you could not bend her from her path, nor lead her back in vain. By these assaults, she has very often been struck and driven by sharp movements and surrounded by various whirlwinds <note>The author uses "whirlwinds" as a metaphor for the chaotic trials and political upheavals the subject faced.</note>. <meta>This page appears to be the concluding section of a Middle French translation of Giovanni Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris" (Concerning Famous Women). [*Miniature: A man in a red robe and white hood sits in a study, reading a book at a wooden lectern. Behind him is a wall with a blue and gold fleur-de-lis pattern. **V**ntil the women of our own time—as is quite apparent—we have now come. Among these, the number of the resplendent <note>Middle French: *resplendissans*. Used here to mean illustrious or famous women.</note> is so very small that I believe it to be more honorable to bring an end to those stories which I have undertaken to put in this work, rather than to proceed further through the introduction and conduct of those of today. And principally, since such a bright and noble queen <note>This likely refers to Joanna I of Naples, the original dedicatee of Boccaccio's work, though the French translator may also be gesturing toward his own patroness.</note> has made and given a conclusion to that which Eve, the first mother of

**[Bottom block text]**

all, began. I know, however, that there will be men who will not fail to say that I have omitted and left out many. And above all others, there are those who will object to other things, who by chance and with good reason might be rebuked themselves. Nevertheless, so that I may respond to the first point with all humility, I confess to having omitted many of them. And certainly, above all else, I could not reach them all. For Time, the conqueror of fame, has taken and consumed many of them; nor was it granted to me to have the power to see all those that remain and are recorded. And of those that are known, I confess that I have passed over and left out many. <note>The author employs a "humility topos," a common medieval literary device where the writer apologizes for their inadequacies or the incompleteness of their work to forestall criticism.</note>

<summary>In this concluding passage, the author explains his decision to end the collection of biographies with his own era's nobility, specifically mentioning a "noble queen." He humbles himself before the reader, acknowledging that many famous women have been lost to the "triumph of Time" or were intentionally omitted from his record.</summary>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page appears to be from the conclusion or a transitional preface of a collection of biographies of famous women, likely a French translation of Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women). The author is discussing his selection process and apologizing for any omissions or errors.</meta>

Both barbarian <note>In the medieval and Renaissance context, 'barbarian' typically referred to non-Greek and non-Roman cultures, such as the Germanic or Eastern tribes.</note>, Greek, and Latin women, as well as the wives of emperors and kings, of whom and of whose deeds I was very well informed. For certainly, I have seen so many of them that they are beyond counting, and I have known their deeds. ¶ But when I took up my pen to weave <note>original: "texir" — The author uses the metaphor of weaving a textile to describe the act of composing the text.</note> this work, it was not my heart’s desire or intent to want to describe them all.

¶ But as I testified from the beginning of this little work, from such a great multitude I would choose to include only a few. ¶ And as I believe this task has been performed by me quite reasonably, any objection that might be raised remains soundly refuted and void. ¶ To others, let it truly be said that I believe it possible—and quite likely—that many other women have been less justly left out of this account.

For certainly, the creator <note>original: "lopperateur" — meaning the author or compiler of the book.</note> is very often deceived, not only by an ignorance of the facts, but by the excessive affection he holds for his own work. ¶ If I have done such a thing, I grieve and repent for it; and I pray, by the venerable honor of honest studies, that more prudent men might bear and endure with good spirit whatever is less well done here. And if in these pages there is any hope <note>The text ends mid-sentence at "esper", likely the beginning of the word "esperance" (hope).</note>

<summary>In this passage, the author reflects on his selection of famous women for his book, admitting that while he could have included many more, he chose a representative few and asks for the reader's indulgence regarding any errors or omissions caused by his own partiality.</summary>

of the aforementioned daughter <note>The original French text uses "mere" (mother), but the context of the legend and the following words "her father" confirm the text refers to the daughter, Camilla.</note> Camilla, her father King Metabus, by a sudden sedition and conspiracy of his citizens, was by them sent into exile, deprived and ousted from his kingdom. And he was constrained to flee into exile, and of all his goods he took nothing with him, except that which he held most dear: this was his aforementioned daughter Camilla, whom he cherished and prized above all things.

¶ In which state <note>original: "In quo"</note>. ¶ And truly the aforementioned king went all alone and on foot into said exile with his daughter in his arms; and when he had arrived at the bank and shore of a river named Amasenus <note>original: "damasenus"; the Amasenus is a river in the Lazio region of Italy.</note>, which, because of the great rains that had fallen from the sky the day before, was excessively swollen and flooded, the said poor and miserable king, thus hindered and burdened by his child, could not pass across and did not know what counsel to take. But God the creator—who did not wish that so noble a virgin (whom he had foreseen would be in time to come most celebrated and virtuous) should perish—provided and placed a timely remedy for said misfortune. 

¶ Her, therefore <note>original: "Illam igitur"</note>. ¶ For the said King Metabus took the bark of trees and wrapped his said daughter within it; and this done, he tied her to a dart or javelin that he held in his hand, and proposed that he would launch his said daughter across the said river. But first he vowed and dedicated her to Diana, promising the goddess that if it pleased her to guard the child and bring her out of said peril, she would be her servant <note>original: "religieuse"; here implying a woman dedicated to the goddess’s service.</note> in perpetual virginity. And this done, he threw with all his strength <note>original: "vertu"; referring to physical power or excellence.</note> his said daughter, thus bound as is said, all across the said river; and suddenly he threw himself into the water and swam after her, so effectively that he saved himself and his said daughter.

¶ And when he found her <note>original: "Et cum illam"</note>. ¶ And after he had perceived and found that his said daughter was safe and reached the shore, he was, amidst his miseries, very joyful; and he entered into the secret places, caves, and hiding spots of the woods and forests, in which, with great misery and labor, he nourished and fed his said daughter on the milk of wild beasts. 

¶ Who, when <note>original: "Que cum"</note>. ¶ Which virgin, after she had come to a mature age, began to cover her body with the skins of beasts which she killed in the hunt. For she began to throw—

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the virtues of maidenhood before transitioning into the history of Penelope, wife of Ulysses. It emphasizes the transition from a protected youth to the "sacrament of marriage."</meta>

...and consider the way of life of the said lady, who in her youth did not spend her time on worldly vanities and displays of wealth, but rather in all labors and hardships to preserve the integrity of her virginity. For she did not lend her ears to the flatteries and deceptive language of the young men who thought to deceive her; she possessed gravity in her speech and had chaste and modest eyes. She was well-composed in her manners and reflected honor in all her gestures. She fled from and avoided all idleness, banquets, dances, and foolish company so that she might observe and keep her said virginity. Thus should young maidens do in their youth, so that after they have become accustomed to virtue and honesty, and have flourished through laudable virginity, they may—at the age of maturity and in obedience to their parents—attain the sacrament of marriage with a title of praise and glory.

And by this means, he delivered his said wife Penelope from the harassments and importunate prayers and requests of the aforementioned men.

She was hardly able to recognize him. But after she had finally recognized him, filled and brimming with unspeakable joy, she very kindly received him whom she had for so long awaited and desired. And they lived the remainder of their lives together in perfect and loyal love.

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Elissa (Dido) following the murder of her husband by her brother, Pygmalion, King of Tyre. The previous text likely established the tension between them and her plan to escape.</meta>

For which reason she was called **Dido**, whereas previously she had been called **Elissa**. And the name **Dido**, translated from the Phoenician language into Latin, signifies the same as a **heroic woman** <note>original: "virago"; in the medieval context, this term referred to a woman possessing masculine strength or spirit</note>. That is to say, one who performs and carries out manly deeds and actions belonging to men. 

The said lady, in order to complete what she had undertaken, approached certain princes and the leading men of the city. These men held the aforementioned **Pygmalion** <note>Pygmalion was the King of Tyre and Dido's brother</note> in hatred and indignation. She persuaded them that if they wished to depart and come away with her, she was determined to go and abandon her said brother, who had most inhumanely killed and put to death her said husband <note>Dido’s husband was Sychaeus, a wealthy priest of Hercules</note>.

<meta>This page continues a discourse on the virtues of Queen Dido of Carthage, specifically focusing on her decision to commit suicide rather than break her vow of widowhood by remarrying. ¶ O Chastity! 
¶ O noble Dido, honor of uncorrupted chastity and modesty! 
¶ Dido, most venerable and eternal resplendent beauty of unbroken widowhood! 

Truly, I wish that the widowed ladies of our time—and above all the Christian ones—would fix the eye of their understanding for a moment to look upon and contemplate your aforementioned integrity and firm purpose of chastity, and that they would consider your constancy and how, to preserve your widowhood and chastity, you were willing to shed your blood. Let those consider it especially who so lightly fly not only to second marriages, but even to third and beyond. 

¶ <note>original: "Quis inquietet"</note> Who will disturb [this peace]? 

¶ I would gladly ask what our ladies will say—those who are signed and marked in their souls with the <term>character of Jesus Christ</term> <note>This refers to the "sacramental character" or indelible mark believed to be left on the soul by baptism, marking the person as belonging to Christ.</note> and who so easily forget their husbands and show by clear sign that they loved them little, by rushing so lightly into third and fourth marriages. What will they answer when they consider a noble and pagan lady, namely the aforementioned Dido <note>The author is following the historical/mythological tradition of Dido as a chaste widow, rather than the more famous version by Virgil in the *Aeneid* where she falls in love with Aeneas.</note>, who had no knowledge of Jesus Christ; and yet, to have some perishable praise after her death, she chose with constant and firm courage to die—not by the hand of another, but by her own hand—rather than rush into a second marriage or promise to violate her most laudable vow that she had made to keep her said widowhood. 

¶ <note>original: "Dicet forsan aliqua"</note> Perhaps some woman will say. 

¶ Some of the Christian ladies might reply and speak against the aforementioned persuasion to keep one's widowhood without flying off to second marriages: "I would do so gladly, but I have been left alone; my parents have passed away; I have such a crowd of marriageable men who press me every day and persuade me with their flatteries and sweet words, which I cannot resist; I am human, and not made of iron or steel." 

¶ <note>original: "O ridiculum"</note> O how ridiculous!

[alone] of all her kin, for the said Lady Dido, for all her help, was in exile / and had an only brother who was her mortal enemy. <note>Dido, the legendary founder of Carthage, fled her home in Tyre after her brother Pygmalion murdered her husband.</note>

She very often said to herself: "Shall I stain my chastity and widowhood <note>In the medieval period, a widow's "chastity"—her vow or decision not to remarry—was considered a state of high moral and spiritual standing.</note> just so that I may provide an heir and owner for my possessions, my city, and my movable goods? O, noble ladies! If you possess great riches, they should be spent and employed wisely. Jesus Christ has many poor people on earth; when you are generous and give these riches to them, you build palaces and heavenly houses in the kingdom of paradise. We are inclined, in order to run toward our pleasures, to lose not only transitory honor, but along with that, eternal honor; and we cast and throw ourselves through carnal pleasure into the pit and abyss of eternal damnation. <meta>This page continues a biographical account of the Queen of Sheba, shifting from her material wealth to her intellectual character and her encounter with King Solomon.</meta>

...wealth so abundant, as has been said, we do not find that she abandoned herself to idleness or feminine softness. Instead, although we do not read who her <term>preceptor</term><note>A private teacher or tutor, typically responsible for the education of royalty or noble children.</note> or master was, this lady was filled with such great <term>science</term><note>In Middle French, "science" refers to broad knowledge, wisdom, and the liberal arts rather than modern experimental science.</note> that it was a marvelous thing to hear. The Holy Scriptures themselves even seem to attest and approve this; by their authority, it is shown that after the Queen of Sheba had heard tell of the wisdom of King Solomon—whose fame flourished and had already filled the whole world—she marveled greatly at it. This is something that fools are not accustomed to do; rather, they despise and scorn those who possess knowledge worthy of praise.

¶ And what is further... <note>original Latin: "Et quod longe"</note>

¶ And certainly, what is more, this lady did not only marvel...

There are those who believe <note>original Latin: "Sunt qui credunt"</note>. Some authors <note>original: "acteurs"</note> opine that this aforementioned queen was named Candace <note>original: "caudacis"</note>, from whose name the kings of Egypt were for a long time called "Candaces."

### The 45th chapter treats of Pamphile. And begins in the text: "A certain Pamphile..." <note>original Latin: "Pamphilam quandem"</note>.

**E** <note>This "E" represents the large illuminated initial in the manuscript</note> I find that Pamphile was a Greek woman, daughter of a man named Plateus <note>original: "platis"</note>. Although she cannot be celebrated with very extensive and clear titles and praises, nevertheless, because she brought some good to the public good <term>public good: the community or the state</term>, she should not be deprived or defrauded of any portion of praise by silence.

For nothing... <note>original Latin: "Nil enim"</note>. And certainly, there is nothing first and newly discovered by anyone—no matter how easy that thing may be to do afterwards—which is not proof of great wit <term>wit: "engin" in Middle French, meaning ingenuity, natural talent, or inventive skill</term> and which should not be considered worthy of glory and praise and numbered among praiseworthy things.

This woman, therefore... <note>original Latin: "Hanc igitur"</note>. Those authors in whom one places faith and credit say that the said Pamphile was the first woman who gathered and assembled the silks and wools that grow on the branches of the trees of the Scythians <note>This likely refers to ancient beliefs regarding the "wool of trees," often confusing cotton or wild silk with plant matter</note>, and specifically gathered the worms that make the said silk. And she first purged and cleaned this silk with the comb, and once this was done, she placed it on a distaff and made thread from it, and afterwards taught how to weave it and put the said silk thread into cloth. This had never been seen before.

Regarding which matter... <note>original Latin: "Cui rei"</note>. By the invention of this thing, one can easily enough recognize and imagine that the very subtle wit of the said lady was very good and very subtle in all other things.

### The 46th chapter treats of Rhea Silvia, and begins in the text: "Rhea, of noble blood." <note>original: "Rhea generosi sanguinis"</note>

[Miniature: A woman in a blue dress (Rhea Silvia) sits on a bed, holding an infant labeled "Romulus". To her right, a woman in a black habit (a Vestal Virgin) holds another infant labeled "Remus". In the background, a person sleeps in a bed. There are two windows in the room.]

**R**hea Silvia was of very noble lineage and shone among the Italians by the brilliance and title of her nobility. For she took and derived her original birth from the Silvii, the kings of Alba <note>Alba Longa, an ancient city in central Italy, southeast of where Rome would be founded</note>. They reigned successively and descended from the noble Aeneas, the leader of the Trojans. 

The said lady was the daughter of Numitor, son of Procas <note>the text says "propoy," a variation of Procas</note> of Alba, who had two sons: namely, Amulius and the said Numitor, father of the said Rhea. To them, the said Procas left his kingdom so that they might hold it alternately. But while this Rhea was still small and of a young age, the said Numitor was chased and ousted from the government of that kingdom by his brother. 

Nevertheless, brotherly pity promised that the said Numitor might live in peace in a certain rural place, to which his brother Amulius sent him as if in exile. And the said Amulius had the son of the said Numitor—a very beautiful youth named Lausus—brought up; and he kept the said Rhea, his niece, who was still small, as has been said. <note>original: "Verum dicitur illi," meaning "But it is said of her"</note>

And so that all hope of lineage <note>meaning any future children who might claim the throne</note> would be taken away from the said Rhea, he had her consecrated and dressed in the habit of the **Vestal Virgins** <note>priestesses of Vesta, the goddess of the hearth, who were legally required to remain virgins for thirty years on pain of death</note> who served in the temple of the goddess Vesta. And he made her a priestess and the principal religious woman of the said temple.

You weep for children abandoned and cast out, and sometimes miserably slain by a most cursed death. You also weep for the ignominious expulsions of these daughters, their flights from the order, their apostasy, and their indecencies. After these miseries, you are sometimes forced to feed and take them back in anyway—all of which you could have easily avoided by marrying them off honorably.

Instead, they must be raised in the houses of their parents and friends from their childhood in all honesty, instructed and taught in good manners until they reach a mature age. This is so that they may know, through integrity of thought, what they ought to do and the state of life in which people wish to place and ordain them. And it is so that of their own free will, and not by force, they may voluntarily take up the burden and responsibility of guarding their perpetual virginity—though I do not believe such women are easily found. It is much better that the number of such virgins be small than to profane the sanctuary of God with an unrestrained multitude of girls who poorly keep their aforementioned virginity, as many have done before now.

Nevertheless, the said queen must be, as far as the title of nobility is concerned, placed and ennobled among the **famous ladies** <note>The French "cleres dames" refers to Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women), a popular 14th-century collection of biographies.</note> because she had sovereignty, lordship, and empire over the people of the said Scythians, who were very cruel, fierce, and untamed.

¶ **Cyrus now** <note>original Latin: "Cirro iam"</note> ¶ When King Cyrus had obtained the kingdom of Asia, in order that he might be better known—striving for glory more than to increase his empire—he coveted the said kingdom of the Scythians. 

¶ **He had heard.** <note>original Latin: "Audierat"</note> ¶ For he had heard it said that the said people were poor people and savage men; but until his time, they had remained undefeated and intact, even though several kings had attacked them and waged war against them. For this reason, he took the desire to lead his army against the said Queen Tomyris, who was a widow. 

¶ **When she had learned of this** <note>original Latin: "Cuius cum pronouisset"</note> ¶ After the said lady had heard news that the said King Cyrus was coming to make war against her—even though the said king was feared and dreaded throughout the world for the marvelous deeds and great acts of valor which he had performed throughout all the lands of Asia, and which were famous and well-known almost throughout the entire world—nevertheless, the said Queen Tomyris did not act like a frightened or cowardly woman who, out of fear and dread, hides herself inside caves. 

Nor did she seek in any way, nor send any message by means of ambassadors, to make peace or any kind of settlement whatsoever with the aforementioned King Cyrus. Instead, as soon as Queen Tomyris had assembled her men-at-arms, captains, and leaders of the army and host, she most quickly and nimbly established herself as the head of the said host like a lady wise and valiant in arms. 

And even though the said Queen Tomyris had in her company several valiant knights and wise captains to properly lead the host and army of the said Queen, nevertheless she did not wish to resist or go out to meet the arrival of the king. Instead, she let him and all his army pass the [river]... <note>Historical accounts, such as those by Herodotus, specify that Tomyris allowed Cyrus to cross the River Araxes into her territory to initiate the battle.</note>

...their good virtues and manners; but along with these, we speak of those who are worthy of memory for whatever cause it may be. The aforementioned Leaina <note>The text uses "Lena," referring to Leaena, a Greek courtesan (hetheira) from the 6th century BCE associated with the Athenian tyrannicides Harmodius and Aristogeiton.</note> did so much through her unchaste life—because she was abandoned as a public woman to carnal pleasures—that her original birth and country are unknown and ignored by all.

*And when they were to be tested* <note>original: "Et cum adprobendos," likely referring to the testing or interrogation of suspects.</note> ¶ And after she had been interrogated, and when through torture and various torments they wanted to compel her to speak and reveal the companions and consorts of the conspiracy for the death of the said tyrant, the aforementioned woman thought to herself that it was a matter of the deed and the venerable name of friendship. And although she was a harlot, almost a public one, nevertheless, after great deliberation within herself—namely, whether she would do any violence to the name of friendship in order to save herself—she concluded with marvelous constancy, commanding her feminine courage <note>The author uses "courage femenin" to highlight her exceptional strength, as medieval texts often viewed "constancy" as a masculine trait.</note> to be firm and constant, and that she should remain silent about that which she was being forced to speak. 

¶ And in effect, she resolved that she would say nothing of the case and that she would accuse no person among them.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Leena (Leaena), an Athenian woman involved in the conspiracy against the tyrants, following her interrogation. It then transitions into Chapter 52 regarding Athalia.</meta>

While [the torments] were increasing <note>original: *Tandū qualescētibꝰ*</note> **And** seeing by the said Leena <note>The Athenian hetaira Leaena, who was tortured for information regarding the assassination of the tyrant Hipparchus</note> that the torments prepared for her were greater than before, she feared that by the force of the torture her physical strength and virtue would be so weakened that she would be forced to change her mind and name those who had committed the deed with her. And by a single noble and unique deed, she took away all hope from the executioners of knowing or extracting from her what they wanted to know through her confession, for which deed she is worthy of perpetual memory. **Who shall say** <note>original: *Quis dicet*</note> **Who** would be the one to say that the said lady lived in or kept brothels <note>original: *bourdeaux*; a reference to her status as a courtesan</note> except by the misfortune and crime of Fate? Certainly, those who say that ladies know not how to keep any secret except what they do not know have not well seen nor known the aforementioned Lady Leena. To save the lives of her friends, by cutting off her tongue, she acquired no less praise than Demosthenes, who through his flourishing and eloquent oratory many times won great praise worthy of perpetual memory.

...and prudent by reducing the wealthy to poverty and [putting] into prison or to death old friends, brothers, sons, nephews, and relatives as if they were mortal enemies.

**No faith** <note>original: "Nulla fides"</note>

In doing this, those who are raised up by such means can preserve neither faith, sanctity, justice, nor law. They lie awake in anguish; they sleep with great difficulty; they neither drink nor eat except in fear. A praiseworthy, desirable, and honorable possession acquired with such great care and concern should certainly rather be called miserable. It would be much better to be a free possessor of some small, poor place and to reside there in peace and security, without anxiety, than to seek and pursue the aforementioned kingdoms and lordships and always be in fear and misery. For the more one seeks these things through the shedding of blood or other devious means, the more one possesses them in great fear. And it often happens that when one trusts in strangers to achieve these lordships through crime and fraud—casting out one's loyal friends and putting oneself entirely into the hands of those strangers—the outcomes of such things are just like their beginnings; for their ends are usually detestable, as appeared in the kingdom of Judah, which was obtained through fraud and injustice by the aforementioned Athaliah <note>Athaliah was a biblical queen of Judah who seized power by murdering the royal family, eventually meeting a violent end.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the war between the early Roman Republic and the Etruscan King Porsenna, transitioning from the story of Mucius Scaevola to the heroic deeds of the Roman maiden Cloelia.</meta>

...Scaevola <note>A Roman youth, Mucius Scaevola, famously attempted to assassinate King Porsenna; his bravery led to peace negotiations.</note>, by which they were forced to withdraw and make peace with the said Romans. ¶ And for the security of the said peace and harmony to be maintained by the said Romans, the said King Porsenna demanded hostages, which was granted to him; among these, the said Virgin Cloelia <note>Spelled "Coclia" in the manuscript; Cloelia was a legendary woman from early Roman history.</note> was delivered to him along with several others.

¶ <gloss>To whom perhaps</gloss> <note>original Latin: "Cui cōforſan"</note> ¶ This Virgin, judging in her own mind that it was a shameful thing for so many noble maidens to be held in the hands of a foreign king, she armed her virginal breast with a great and manly courage. And she found a way to deceive the guards who had charge over her, for she managed to escape from their hands. And finding a horse grazing on the bank of the Tiber—and despite the fact that she had never before mounted a horse—nonetheless, in the middle of the night and in the presence of several other maidens whom she had drawn out and brought to the edge of the said river, she mounted the said horse without being in any way terrified or frightened by the depth of the river, nor by the perilous whirlpools and currents through which she had to pass. And in fact, she ferried across several of the said maidens <note>The text suggests she helped other girls across, though most historical accounts emphasize her leading them in a swim.</note>, whom she restored to their parents.

<gloss>What Porsenna [did]</gloss> <note>original Latin: "Quod porſenna"</note> When the said King Porsenna perceived and heard of this deed, he complained to the Roman Senate that the Romans were not keeping their promises and intended to deceive him. When the Senate heard this, it was ordered and agreed that the said Virgin Cloelia, who had undertaken this feat, should be sent back to King Porsenna—provided that the King would return and restore her to the Romans as a maiden, chaste and unharmed, without doing her any injury. This was done.

¶ <gloss>But the King</gloss> <note>original Latin: "Rex autem"</note> ¶ The aforementioned King Porsenna, considering and marveling within himself at the virtue of the said Virgin, and being greatly pleased by her boldness, did not only grant her the leave and power to return to her parents; he also gave her the authority to take with her all those she wished to choose from among those held as hostages...

<meta>This page continues the historical accounts of famous Romans, focusing here on the transition of Gnaeus Marcius from a Roman hero to an exiled commander of Rome's enemies.</meta>

...named Gnaeus Marcius, a man of most excellent virtue, quick and ready to give good counsel and even better at performing it with his own hand. At the time when the Romans had besieged a place named Corioli in the land of the Volscians, and when this place had been captured through the virtue and prowess of the said Marcius, the Romans—out of respect for such great nobility and virtue—gave him a surname taken from the name of that place, Corioli; for he was named Coriolanus. It was promised to this Gnaeus Marcius Coriolanus by the Romans that he could undertake any duty concerning the well-being and benefit of the Roman state.

### For which reason
<note>original: "Quam obrem"</note>

A certain time afterward, the city of Rome suffered a great need for grain. During this time, through the diligence of the "Fathers" <note>The *Patres*, or Senators</note> and Roman citizens, a large quantity of grain was brought from Sicily. Coriolanus forbade any part of this grain from being delivered to the common people until he had ordered otherwise.

### Against whom they were hostile
<note>original: "In quem infesta"</note>

The common people, who were almost entirely starving, would have laid hands on him violently if a day had not been assigned to Coriolanus by the Tribune of the People <note>An official elected to protect the interests of the plebeians</note> to speak and present the reasons why he wanted to prevent the wheat from being given and delivered to the people.

### Who, when he [did not obey]
<note>original: "Qui cum non..."</note>

Coriolanus would not obey or submit to this ordinance. For this reason, he was condemned and sent into exile. In effect, he departed and went to surrender himself to the Volscians, who not long before had been enemies of the Romans. Coriolanus was very kindly and honorably received by these Volscians, for virtue is prized everywhere.

### These men
<note>original: "Hos iste"</note>

The aforementioned Coriolanus and Attius Tullius the Volscian <note>A leader of the Volscians and Coriolanus's host</note> used such trickery that they stirred up all the Volscians to wage war against the Romans. The Volscians appointed Coriolanus as the chief and commander <term>General (original: "empereur") <gloss>At this time, "empereur" referred to a military commander (imperator) rather than a monarch.</gloss></term> of their army. In effect, he guided and led this army to within a stone's throw <note>original: "iet de quatre pierres" — literally 'a throw of four stones', meaning a very short distance</note> of the city of Rome.

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative concerning Queen Artemisia II of Caria and the construction of the Mausoleum at Halicarnassus, one of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World, intended as a monument to her late husband, King Mausolus.</meta>

At that time, Greece was considered to have the best craftsmen in the world. According to the orders and advice of these masters, she had them construct a tomb made of stones of well-cut and appropriate marble, so that through this tomb, the name of her late husband might be eternal, if it could not be preserved in any other way.

¶ **Of which indeed** <note>original Latin: "Cuius quidem"</note> ¶ Because this burial place—as much through art as through the intricacy, sumptuousness, and expense involved—surpassed almost all other buildings and has long been reputed as one of the seven wonders of the world, it will not be an absurd thing to have made special mention of it. For the fame of the craftsmen will flourish and be honored, and the magnificence of the said noble lady will gain even greater renown.

¶ **Therefore the architects** <note>original Latin: "Architecti igitur"</note> ¶ The masters of this work built, by order of the said queen, near Halicarnassus <note>The capital of Caria, in modern-day Turkey</note>, the principal city of Caria, a tomb in a square shape. Looking at the faces and figures of the said tomb toward the regions of the south and north:

They directed and conducted the said work to a length of sixty-three feet <note>original: "lxiii. pies"</note>, and the other sides they made shorter. The work was raised to a height of one hundred and forty feet <note>original: "cent xl. pies"</note>. And so that it might be completely enclosed, they surrounded it with thirty-six columns of Memphian stones <note>original: "pierres membraines," likely referring to a specific high-quality stone described by ancient authors like Pliny</note>.

¶ **Furthermore, that part** <note>original Latin: "Ceterum eam partem"</note> ¶ Afterward, they say that Scopas <note>A famous Greek sculptor and architect</note> had carved and completed that part facing toward the east. And Bryaxis <note>A Greek sculptor of the 4th century BCE</note> completed that which faces toward the north <note>original: "borias ceſt galerne," referring to the north or northwest wind</note>, just as Leochares <note>An Athenian sculptor</note> had undertaken the western part. The fourth side, toward the south, was left to Timotheus <note>A celebrated sculptor of the same era</note>.

¶ **Who in sculpting** <note>original Latin: "Qui in ſculpendis"</note> ¶ These masters, in carving the statues and images, the histories <note>narrative reliefs or scenes</note>, and other pleasant and delightful things belonging to the work, expressed and exerted with such great skill and subtlety the forces of their ingenuity—each of them desiring in his mastery and knowledge to surpass the others—that from the marble stones it seemed they had brought forth and made faces that were completely alive. This has been noted in former times by those who have looked...

<summary>This page describes the construction and architectural details of the Mausoleum of Halicarnassus, highlighting the work of famous Greek sculptors like Scopas and Leochares. It details the dimensions, the thirty-six columns, and the exceptional lifelike quality of the marble carvings.</summary>

...and grown. And not only then, but for a very long time afterward, it appeared that the hands of the said craftsmen had striven and debated over this for the glory of their reputation.

¶ **Nor did it happen** <note>original: "Nec contigit"</note>

¶ Nor could Artemisia, because of the death that took her from this life, see this noble work perfected. Nevertheless, the said craftsmen did not leave their works unfinished; rather, thinking that these labors of theirs would be a most certain instruction and witness of their <term>engine|ingenuity and artistic skill</term> for those who would come after them in the future, they conducted and brought what they had started to its conclusion.

¶ **But there was added** <note>original: "Sed accessit"</note>

¶ Moreover, there arrived a fifth master of this work named Pytheas <note>original: "ptherō"; likely referring to the architect Pythius of Priene</note> who raised the height of the highest pyramid and made it equal by twenty-four steps. To these aforementioned masters was added the sixth carver named Pythis <note>original: "Piches"; likely the same Pythius or a collaborator</note>, by whose labor a marble chariot was added to the summit and highest point of that entire edifice. Upon this so rich and excellent work, finished and completed, the name of "Mausoleum" was bestowed, from the name of King Mausolus <note>original: "manseolus"</note> for whom it was made; and from this name, as the most worthy, the tombs of subsequent kings were called "mausoleums."

¶ **Therefore, renowned** <note>original: "Clarus ergo"</note>

¶ Clear and most noble was the love of Artemisia, and even clearer were the perseverance of her widowhood and her tears. And if by chance the said lady often took up arms, nevertheless we have read that—her tears having ceased a long time after—she took up these arms twice.

...preservation of the country. And secondly, so that she might keep the faith required of a society. With the certain hope of being able to seize that kingdom, they came there suddenly to occupy it.

¶ Now the city of Halicarnassus is situated on the sea of Caria in a place naturally fortified and strong, having two ports. One of these, which is called the smaller one, is inside the city with a narrow entrance and is, as it were, hidden <note>original: "muce"; from the Middle French 'mucier', meaning to conceal or hide.</note>. It is situated in such a way that from the royal palace overlooking this port, all necessary and opportune things can be prepared and brought in without being seen by any of the citizens, nor even by strangers, by those who guard the palace. The other port, which is the larger one, is situated on the open sea near the walls of the city.

¶ **Into which** <note>original Latin: "In quem"</note> ¶ When Artemisia knew the Rhodians had arrived, she commanded her people to prepare themselves and keep to their arms. And she commanded the sailors she had assembled, along with provisions, armor, and other equipment suitable to accomplish the task she had conceived in her heart. Likewise, she commanded her citizens that when she gave them a signal, they should applaud and welcome the Rhodians, calling to them from atop the walls to give them hope of surrender and of delivering the city to them; and she ordered that, if they could, they should lead them into the marketplace.

¶ **Immediately after** <note>original Latin: "Demū euestigio"</note> ¶ In whatever manner the thing was done, Artemisia—without the Rhodian enemies noticing—departed from the smaller port with her men and went out into the open sea. And when she perceived that, after the signal she had given, the Rhodians were called in by the citizens, and that the said Rhodians, as if victorious, took their course and path toward the marketplace, the said Artemisia with her ship attacking <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with her ambush of the Rhodian fleet.</note>

¶ The Rhodians, seeing from their watchtowers <term>eschauguettes: small overhanging turrets on a fortification used for lookout</term> their ships thus adorned with branches, and believing by this sign that their people had obtained the victory, opened their port and the gates of their city, not knowing what had happened. And in place of their own citizens, they received the said victorious queen. And thus their city was suddenly occupied by Artemisia.

¶ The lady, as a sign of victory and triumph, commanded that two bronze <term>derain: an old French term for brass or bronze</term> statues be erected in public, one of which represented the figure of the victorious Artemisia, and the other represented the city of Rhodes being defeated. And after she had placed and set up the aforementioned signs and figures in memory of what she had done, and after she had made the island of the said place of Rhodes tributary to her, she returned to her city.

¶ **Furthermore, with various other matters** <note>original Latin: *Preterea cum diuersis*</note> ¶ Moreover, when Xerxes, the most powerful king of Persia, had filled the lands and countries with the armies and companies of his infantry against the Lacedaemonians <note>The Spartans and their allies</note>, and when he had occupied all the shores with his great navy so that, according to his judgment, he might not only take but absorb and destroy all of Greece, Queen Artemisia, at his request, joined that war. In which battle the said Ar— <note>The text cuts off mid-name; she is famously noted by Herodotus for her bravery at the Battle of Salamis</note>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of famous women from antiquity, focusing here on the Roman maiden Virginia, a story typically used to illustrate the transition from the tyranny of the Decemvirs back to the Roman Republic.</meta>

...cherished in sweet memory, for she was adorned with very noble beauty. She was the daughter of Aulus Virginius, a very honest <term>plebeian</term> <note>A member of the common people of Rome, as opposed to the aristocratic patricians.</note> man. 

### What she was
And although she was of very good character <note>original: "idole," referring to her natural disposition or innate virtue</note>, she was nonetheless not so much renowned for her own constancy as she was for the wickedness of a miserable lover and for a very cruel deed perpetrated upon her by her father, from which the liberty of the Romans followed. 

### This indeed <note>original: "Hec equidem"</note>
The said Virginia, at the time when the said men <note>The Decemvirs, a group of ten men appointed to codify Roman law who eventually became despotic.</note> ruled for the second year in which they held power in the city of Rome, was betrothed by her said father to Lucius Atilius of the order of the tribunes. And because Virginius was by chance serving as a soldier in the military expedition undertaken at <term>Algidus</term> <note>Mount Algidus, a frequent site of conflict in central Italy.</note> by the Romans against the <term>Aequi</term> <note>An ancient italic tribe frequently at war with early Rome.</note>, he had delayed the wedding of his said daughter. 

### While things were so <note>original: "Quibus sic se habentibus"</note>
While things were in this state, it happened by Virginia's fortune that Appius Claudius—one of the ten men who had remained alone of all his companions (who were then away on campaign) to guard the city along with Spurius Appius—cast his eyes upon the beauty of the said Virginia. She pleased him so much that he loved her with a foolish passion. 

The said Virgin, being still of tender age, rejected his flatteries. He took no account of her great prayers or threats; for her heart, imbued and showered with holiness, would not bend for anything. Because of this, the said Appius was inflamed with such fury that after he had turned and revolved his thoughts, wavering in various directions, and considering that to use public force against the said Virgin would not be a safe thing, he turned his ingenuity to the use of fraud. 

And by a scheme devised within himself, he arranged so that Marcus Claudius—his <term>freedman</term> <note>A person transition from slavery to freedom who often remained a client of their former master.</note> of prime audacity—would seize and take the said Virginia (who occasionally passed near the market) and lead her to his house as his slave and fugitive <term>chattel</term> <note>original: "mancipe"; a legal term for a piece of property or a person under the legal hand of another</note> as soon as he could find the occasion and place to do so. And if anyone by chance wished to hinder his enterprise, they should immediately be summoned to appear in court before him. 

### Whom... <note>original: "Quam"</note>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Virginia's legal struggle against the Roman decemvir Appius Claudius, moving from her initial seizure to the tragic climax of the story.</meta>

**When a few** <note>original Latin: *cum paucos*</note> **And** as, a few days later, the aforementioned freedman <note>Marcus Claudius, acting as a pawn for the corrupt judge Appius Claudius.</note> with presumptuous boldness had seized this **Maiden** while she was passing by, claiming she was his own, Virginia cried out and resisted with all her might against the aforementioned man, the worst of men. The matrons with whom she was walking helped her as best they could. Suddenly a great crowd of men ran to the scene, among whom arrived her betrothed, **Icilius** <note>The manuscript identifies him as "Attilius," likely a scribal error for the historical Icilius.</note>. And after many words were spoken by each of the parties, the matter came to such an end and conclusion that Virginia was brought to the courthouse before the judge—her very pursuer <note>The judge was Appius Claudius, the man who had orchestrated the kidnapping to satisfy his own lust.</note>. And it was only with difficulty obtained from the said Appius, who was burning with love for her, that the judgment be deferred until the next day. 

On that day, his trickery profited him little; by this fraud he had sent word to the army camp to inform the generals and captains that if **Virginius** <note>Virginia's father, a centurion serving with the Roman army.</note> asked for leave to return to Rome, it should by no means be permitted. However, the said Virginius was quickly recalled by the captains <note>In the historical narrative, Virginius's supporters reached him before Appius’s messengers could deliver the order to detain him.</note>. Nevertheless, the said Virginius, father of Virginia, departed and came to Rome; he came into court with his daughter, his other friends, and with Icilius, who was in mourning and soiled clothes <note>In ancient Rome, those under legal threat or in mourning wore dirty, unkept clothing (*sordidatus*) to gain public sympathy.</note>. 

And there, **Marcus**, their opposing party, demanded his <term>mancipe<gloss>a legal term for a person held as property, such as a slave or ward</gloss></term>; and she, by the lustful presiding judge Appius Claudius—who refused to hear Virginius—was judged to be a fugitive slave belonging to the said Marcus Claudius. When Marcus tried to take her, and Virginius her father had spoken many words in vain against Appius, it was finally granted to the said Virginius (who was gnashing his teeth in rage) that he might speak a little to her and her nurse, so that if perhaps the truth of the "old error" <note>Referring to Appius's false claim that Virginia was not Virginius's daughter but a slave's child swapped at birth.</note> were discovered, he might deliver the <term>mancipe</term> to Marcus with less shame. 

**And when with them** <note>original Latin: *cumque cum eis*</note> **And** after he had retired with them near the **Cloacine shops** <note>Specifically the shops near the shrine of Venus Cloacina in the Roman Forum.</note> in view of the court, he drew a knife which he had hidden and said these words: "My dearest daughter, I defend your liberty in the only way I can." And having said this, he thrust the full blade of the knife into the breast of his daughter **Virginia**.

## **Olympias**

**Olympias**, Queen of the **Macedonians**, was by the accumulation of titles and praises most noble and of great renown. For first, if the ornaments of generations and lineages can bring to mortals any light of nobility, the said **Olympias** took and held her original birth from the blood of the **Aeacides** <term>Aeacides: The descendants of Aeacus, a prestigious Greek lineage that included the hero Achilles, from whom Olympias claimed descent</term>, which then, above all others in **Greece**—indeed, in the whole world—was the most noble and resplendent. 

She was the daughter of **Neoptolemus**, King of the **Molossians** <note>The Molossians were the principal tribe of Epirus, in modern-day northwestern Greece and Albania</note>; and although in her childhood she was called **Myrtale** <note>original: "missilis"; likely a scribal error or variant for Myrtale, the name Olympias bore as a child</note>, nevertheless after she was married to the most noble Prince **Philip**, King of **Macedon**, she was then for the first time called **Olympias**.

¶ Furthermore <note>original: "Preterea"</note> ¶ 

In addition to this, she had a brother named **Alexander**, King of **Epirus**. And she had a son named **Alexander** <note>Alexander the Great</note>, whose deeds were so very great that there has not been until now, nor shall there be hereafter, any man whose deeds could or can surpass in the glory of fame those of the said **Alexander**, even though the said glory is a vain thing and of little profit. The said **Alexander** added no small amount of splendor to his said mother **Olympias**; truly, if having given birth to a son of great virtue and magnanimity is a resplendent thing. 

For that brightness could not entirely allow her to escape being sometimes blackened and obscured by a mark and stain cast upon her. And by this mark and stain she was most wickedly renowned. ¶ For in the flowering age of her youth, she was tainted and soiled by vile...

**B**ehold how easily Claudia, a Vestal Virgin <note>A priestess of Vesta, the Roman goddess of the hearth. Acting no differently than if he were against a man unworthy of having such an honor, the tribune stood in the middle of the path to block him; and with a high and proud audacity in the manner of a tribune, laying his hands upon the triumphing man, he strove to throw him out of his chariot.

Compelled by piety, moved with sadness, and having forgotten her sex and the modesty of her veil and the garments in which she was dressed and covered, she could not bear to restrain herself from rushing impetuously into the midst of the gathering. By a bold effort, she forced the crowd <term>turbe</term> <note>From the Latin *turba*, meaning a confused or unruly crowd.</note> to make way for her. With unbelievable vigor, she stepped between the arrogance of the tribune and the glory of her father; and by whatever bold acts she performed, the tribune was thus pushed back by her, giving her father free entry to the Capitol <note>The Capitoline Hill, the destination of Roman triumphal processions where sacrifices were made at the Temple of Jupiter.</note>. **O** sweet love! <note>original Latin: *Dulcis amor*</note> O unfettered piety! --- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of a woman sentenced to death by starvation, focusing on the extraordinary devotion of her daughter. The text follows the tradition of Valerius Maximus's "Memorable Deeds and Sayings."</meta>

...to be consumed there by famine. To visit her, her daughter came, who was first thoroughly searched to ensure she did not carry any food to her mother; however, she obtained permission from the guard to enter the prison. And her mother, already constrained by hunger, was rescued and suckled by the milk of her breasts, with which she was then overflowing due to her recent childbirth. 

¶ Finally, as this continued <note>original: "Demum continuatis"</note> ¶ This matter having continued for a few days, the guard began to marvel that this condemned woman could live or retain her spirit without having eaten anything. And secretly he observed and perceived what the daughter was doing for her mother, and how she offered her breasts to nurse and suckle. And he, being thus amazed by the <term>filial piety</term> <note>The text uses "pitie," which here refers to the Latin "pietas," meaning duty and devotion to one's parents and gods rather than just "mercy."</note> and by the unusual method the daughter had found to nourish her mother, reported this matter to the <term>triumvir</term> <note>A Roman magistrate; in this context, likely the "triumviri capitales" who oversaw prisons.</note>. That triumvir announced it to the <term>praetor</term> <note>A high-ranking Roman official responsible for the administration of justice.</note> or the public council, whereby by the common consent of all, it was granted that the daughter was given a pardon for the punishment deserved by her mother. 

¶ If to the one who saves... <note>original: "Si servanti"</note> ¶ If it is so that a <term>crown of oak</term> <note>The "corona civica," one of the highest military honors in Rome, awarded to a soldier who saved the life of a fellow citizen.</note> was given to he who, through his virtue in war, saved a Roman citizen, with what crown shall we decorate this daughter who, with her milk, has saved and nourished her mother in the prison? Certainly, I do not believe that for such a pious deed one could find a worthy enough wreath among the branches and leaves of trees. This piety was not only holy, but most marvelous; not only to be equaled, but to be preferred to the gift of nature. For nature teaches us to nourish and raise children with milk, but this aforementioned piety teaches us to rescue our parents from death. Marvelous, then, are the forces of filial devotion.

<meta>This page begins the biography of Marcia (often identified with the historical Iaia of Cyzicus), a celebrated female artist of antiquity. It follows the standard format of Boccaccio's "Of Famous Women," focusing on both her moral character and her professional skill.</meta>

[Miniature: A woman in a red dress and white apron is sculpting a reclining statue on a wooden table using a mallet and chisel. Another wooden table with tools sits in the foreground.]

**Marcia Varro**, a perpetual virgin, lived long ago in Rome. However, I have no memory of having read of which Varro she was the daughter, nor in what time she lived. I consider the virginity she observed to deserve to be all the more exalted with greater praise because she, of her own free will and full intent, without any constraint, chose to keep it with great integrity. For the said Marcia was neither bound nor obligated by the vows of Diana <note>Diana was the Roman goddess of the hunt and a symbol of chastity; her followers or priestesses often took formal vows of virginity.</note> nor hindered by any other profession <note>Likely referring to the Vestal Virgins or later Christian monastic orders.</note> by which many virgins have been constrained and kept in their calling. Rather, by the sole integrity of her spirit, she overcame the sting of the flesh <note>A common medieval reference to "the prick of the flesh" or sexual temptation.</note>—to which many very noble men have succumbed—and she kept her body until death clean and pure, without the stain of a man's touch.

¶ *But even if by this* <note>original: "Verum et si hac"</note> ¶ Truly, if Marcia is to be praised for such commendable constancy, she is nonetheless no less to be recommended for the strength of her talent <term>talent: the original French "engin" refers to innate intellectual power, ingenuity, or creative wit</term> and the skill of her hands. For whether she learned under some master or nature led her to it, so that she would not waste away in idleness, she gave herself entirely to the study of painting and carving. And she painted so skillfully and with such polish with her brush, and carved images in ivory, that she surpassed Sopolis and Dionysius <note>Two famous Greek painters of the Hellenistic period mentioned by Pliny the Elder.</note>, who were very famous painters in her time. And of this, there was very well-approved evidence and witness that the panels painted by her were the most...

### Chapter 49 treats of Harmonia and begins in the text: "Harmonia of Sicily."
<note>original: "armonia sicula"</note>

**Harmonia**, a young Sicilian girl, was the daughter of Gelon, brother of Hiero, King of the Syracusans. Although she was ennobled by royal lineage, she is much more worthy of memory because of the devotion <note>The original term "pitié" (pietas) implies a sense of duty and sacrificial love for one's family and country.</note> that was found in her. Some maintain that she died a virgin; others say she was the wife of a man named Themistus; one may choose whichever of these two versions pleases them most.

<note>original: "Cum igitur syracusanorum..."</note> When, therefore, the people of Syracuse—through a supreme, sudden, and blinded rebellion—cruelly raged against the entire royal line; and once Hieronymus <note>The OCR reads "iherosme," referring to the young king of Syracuse assassinated in 214 BCE.</note>, their king (then a youth), and Andranodorus and Themistus, the royal sons-in-law, had been slain by them; and when a tumultuous rush was made against Demarata and Heraclia (daughters of Hieronymus) and Harmonia (daughter of Gelon) by the common people with their swords drawn: it happened, through the wisdom of Harmonia’s nurse, that a young girl dressed in royal robes and of the same age as Harmonia was prepared and delivered to the murderers in her place. This girl was in no way opposed or contrary to the will and desire of the nurse who ainsi prepared and delivered her. <meta>This page continues the narrative of Harmonia of Syracuse, who was being hidden from a murderous mob by her nurse. The previous page would have described the nurse’s plan to dress another girl in Harmonia’s clothes to deceive the assassins.</meta>

...[those who] struck her, she did not confess her condition <note>That is, her social status or identity as a commoner rather than the princess.</note> nor who she was. Nor did Harmonia—who was hidden, and in whose place the girl was being killed—accuse her. Rather, truly, that girl remained silent and without moving while receiving the mortal blows, until she came to her death. Because of this, Harmonia was at once honored and miserable due to the loss of a girl who was so faithful <note>original: "feable", meaning "feal" or "loyal".</note> toward her.

However, as Harmonia saw from the secret or hidden place where she was the perseverance of the innocent maiden, her great courage in the face of death, and the blood flowing from her wounds, she was stunned by it. The said maiden was now dead and the persecutors had already departed from that place; Harmonia could have escaped death, but she began to marvel with all her thoughts at the faith <note>In this context, "faith" means "loyalty" or "fidelity".</note> of that maiden. Overtaken by a very noble pity <term>pity (Middle French: "pitie")<gloss>This term often carries the weight of the Latin "pietas," implying not just compassion but a sense of sacred duty or devotion to one's family and gods.</gloss></term>, and shedding tears, she could not bear to look upon that innocent blood remaining unavenged, nor to prolong her own life into old age through such a deed and at the cost of another's loyalty. 

Judging it more fitting to descend into the underworld <note>original: "en enfer", literally "into hell," but referring here to the classical Hades.</note> with such a faithful young girl by a premature and untimely death, rather than waiting for the age of white-haired old age among disloyal citizens. Oh pity! Oh primal faith! Such as the ancient fathers were accustomed to practice, and which Harmonia wished to use by throwing herself into the midst of the crowd of her murderers, from whose bloody swords she had just escaped. By recounting the trickery of her nurse, the loyalty of the maiden, and her own true identity, she abandoned her blood to the funerary offerings <term>offerings (Middle French: "inferies")<gloss>From the Latin "inferiae," these were sacrifices or offerings made to the souls of the deceased.</gloss></term> and the death of that maiden. 

And after she had been wounded by many blows, she let herself fall in death beside the body of the maiden who had already passed away. To her, that which pity took from her lifetime has been restored by literature <note>original: "par lettres", meaning her story has been preserved in writing for posterity.</note>. And certainly, the loyalty of the first maiden and the pity of the second make their names everlasting.

### And begins in the text: Beronice of Pontus, who was also called Laodice. <note>original: "Beronices pontica cui & leodaces"</note>

This Beronice was the daughter of that Mithridates, King of Pontus, who a little while before had waged war with the Romans against King Aristonicus <note>Aristonicus (Eumenes III) was a pretender to the throne of Pergamum who fought Roman expansion.</note>, which Mithridates had been removed by a sudden death. She was also the sister of Mithridates <note>Mithridates VI Eupator, known as "The Poison King," one of the Roman Republic’s most formidable enemies.</note>, son of the aforementioned king, and she married Ariarathes, King of **Cappadocia**. When this Ariarathes was killed in treason by a man named Gordius, at the instigation of the said Mithridates, two surviving sons remained. 

It then happened that Nicomedes, at that time King of **Bithynia**, occupied the kingdom of Cappadocia as if it were empty and vacant due to the death of the said king. Seeing this, Mithridates coveted the kingdom for himself and feigned having pity for his nephews, claiming he wanted to recover the said kingdom for them, and in effect took...

arms against Nicomedes. When he learned that the widow Laodice, his sister, had married Nicomedes, his feigned pity was converted into true pity; <note>original: "sa pitie sainte fut en vraye pitie conuertie." The author suggests Mithridates' initial "holy" or outward show of concern for his sister's family became a genuine, albeit violent, motivation once his rival Nicomedes became involved.</note> for he restored Ariarathes, the elder of the two brothers, to his paternal kingdom and drove Nicomedes out of the said kingdom of Cappadocia by force of arms. But after this, he repented of this action and caused the said Ariarathes, his nephew, and his younger brother—who was also named Ariarathes—to be put to death.

The unhappy mother, thus widowed and stripped of her two sons, bore the aforementioned killings so grievously and with such ill patience that, constrained by grief and having forgotten her sex, and entirely furious, she took up arms, mounted a chariot harnessed with horses, and following at a very swift pace, did not cease to pursue a man named Leneus, a henchman <term>satellite: from the Latin 'satelles,' meaning an attendant, guard, or subordinate official acting on behalf of a superior.</term> of her brother Mithridates.

This Leneus had been the executioner of that wicked murder; she pursued him until she had pierced him with her lance and, with a blow from a stone, cast him dead to the ground. She then made her car <term>curre: an archaic term for a chariot or heavy carriage.</term> and chariot pass over his dead body amidst the darts and blows of her enemies, without any fear of her said brother, now her enemy. And she did so much that she arrived at the house where she believed the body of her dead son was being kept, upon which she shed maternal tears and performed his funeral rites.

**O** good God! O unconquerable <note>original: "expugnables." While the word literally means "conquerable," in this rhetorical context it refers to the overwhelming, driving forces of natural instinct.</note> forces of nature and of merciless love, which have lent ingenuity and boldness to that woman to kill the aforementioned murderer, despite the strength and hatred of the king—so fearsome to others—being utterly despised by her.
