# The Wife of Ortiagon

The wife <note>Her name is Chiomara, though the author notes it was often omitted in earlier records.</note> of **Ortiagon**, a petty king of the **Galatians** <note>original: "galogrecz"; a Celtic people living in central Gaulish-Greek Asia Minor.</note>, seems to have lost the well-deserved honor and the principal reward of her merit because her name remained unknown. This name was obscured by the barbarian envy of that nation among the Mediterranean rocks and caverns of Asia, and thus kept closed and hidden from the Latin people.

¶ But let it not happen that her merit and the splendor of her fame should not be restored to her, at least as far as our humble letters are able.

¶ **Therefore, having been overcome** <note>original: "Superato igitur"</note>

¶ For after **Antiochus the Great**, king of Asia and Syria, had been defeated by the Romans under the leader Scipio, [Gnaeus] **Manlius Torquatus** <note>The text names him Torquatus, but history identifies him as Gnaeus Manlius Vulso, the consul who led the Galatian War in 189 BC.</note>—to whose lot the province of Asia had fallen—led his forces onward. He did this so that he might not seem to have transported his vast multitudes of people in vain, and so that he might not keep his knights idle. Once he had wiped out the remnants and survivors of the enemies near the coastal regions according to his will, he led and conducted his army into the wild and hidden regions of Asia. There, he waged a very bitter war and battle against the Galatians, a people of unbridled and very cruel barbarian nature, because they had aided Antiochus against the Roman arms and had very often troubled all of Asia with their raids.

¶ **But when now** <note>original: "Verum cū iam"</note>

¶ When the Galatians, who now despaired of resisting, had abandoned their cities and homes and had gone with their wives, children, and other goods to the summits and heights...

<meta>This page serves as the opening of the manuscript, introducing the work as a translation of Giovanni Boccaccio’s "De mulieribus claris" (Concerning Famous Women). The text establishes a connection between Boccaccio and his contemporary, Petrarch.</meta>

# The first chapter is the prologue of the said Boccaccio.

*[Miniature: A scholar, likely Boccaccio, sits in a study at a desk with books, gesturing toward three women standing before him.]*

**A**ncient authors <note>original: "acteurs." In the Middle Ages, this term referred specifically to authoritative classical writers.</note> have long since written, briefly and succinctly, certain books and treatises on the deeds of noble men. Nevertheless, in our time, Francesco Petrarch <note>Petrarch (1304–1374) was a famous Italian poet and scholar. Boccaccio is referring to Petrarch's book "On Illustrious Men."</note>—our teacher and master, a noble and distinguished man—has worthily written a more extensive volume in a more ornate style and with greater eloquence.

**More than others** <note>original: "Ma q̄ ceteros" (Latin: *Magis quam ceteros*). This phrase emphasizes that Petrarch surpassed previous writers in his historical accounts.</note>

**A**nd truly, those who have applied and employed all their study, their wealth, their blood, and their soul—whenever it was needed and necessary—to perform and perfect virtuous and magnificent works so that they might surpass all others, have reasonably earned, through these aforementioned deeds, that their names...

<summary>This page introduces Boccaccio’s "Concerning Famous Women," acknowledging that while many have written about famous men—including his mentor Petrarch—there is a tradition of honoring those who sacrifice everything for virtue.</summary>
<keywords>Boccaccio, Petrarch, Prologue, Virtue, Fame, Noble Men, Classical Authors</keywords>

--- Page 215 ---
<meta>This page appears to be the fourth leaf (signature a iiii) of a preface or prologue to a collection of biographies of famous women, likely a French translation of Giovanni Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women). The text continues from a previous page discussing how previous authors failed to specifically record the achievements of women.</meta>

...of them has pursued or described in particular the grace and praise-worthy memory of the said women. 

**A**ntending—since it appears clearly through many histories—that some of the said ladies have done and perfected certain things no less strongly nor less **manfully** <note>original: "virilement"; in this period, "manliness" was a term of high praise for any person showing courage, regardless of gender</note> than the noble and brave men, for which by right they ought to be praised and **extolled** as much as the said virtuous men. For if the aforementioned men, who have done and perfected great and magnificent things, are praised and proclaimed by reason of their aforementioned works, then by much stronger reason the women and noble ladies—to whom nature has given a soft and frail sex and condition, a weak body, and a **slow wit** <note>original: "engin tardif"; the author reflects the common medieval prejudice that women were naturally less intellectually capable, making their achievements seem more miraculous</note>—should be elevated, praised, and extolled if by adventure the aforementioned ladies escape their condition and are able to have a **manly courage** <note>original: "courage virile"</note> and a subtle and celebrated **intellect** <note>original: "engin"</note>, by which they have the boldness to perfect through their virtue, courage, and wit the aforementioned things which would be very difficult to do even for powerful and virtuous men.

**E**t ideo ne merito <note>original Latin: "And therefore not without merit"</note>. **A**t this cause, so that the said women might not be entirely deprived and defrauded of their said merits, it has fallen into my understanding to assemble and redact into one volume, to their glory and honor, the noble deeds of those who occur to my memory. And to add among the aforementioned some from the number of those who, by their boldness, strength, virtue, wit, industry, gift of nature, or of fortune, have been made notable and worthy of praise and memory. And I will likewise add the deeds of some women who, although they themselves have not done anything worthy of memory, nevertheless were the **cause** of very great and memorable deeds.

**N**ec mirum <note>original Latin: "Nor is it a wonder"</note>. **E**t it should not be strange or incongruous to the readers if they find **Medea** <note>A figure from Greek mythology known for her powerful but dark sorcery</note> and **Flora** <note>Likely referring to the Roman goddess or the legendary Roman courtesan</note>, or other similar women who had great intellect, even if it was very damaging and pernicious, mingled with the chaste, brave, and loyal women.

->a iiii<-

<summary>This page serves as an introduction to a biographical collection of famous women, arguing that women's achievements are more impressive than men's because they must overcome a "frail nature." The author explains his intent to include both virtuous and "pernicious" women (like Medea) whose intellect made them notable.</summary>

<keywords>Famous Women, Boccaccio, Medea, Flora, virtue, manly courage, feminine intellect, biography, Middle French literature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Ops (the Roman goddess of the earth) and her role in protecting her children from Saturn. It transitions from mythological origins to the historical Roman adoption of her cult during the Punic Wars.</meta>

...because she conceived three children by the said Saturn—namely Jupiter, Pluto, and Neptune. These were men of great and marvelous deeds. And through her cleverness, she delivered them from the hand and persecution of their father Saturn, who devoured those he had fathered. <note>original: "Qui cum" — Who with...</note>

**I**hese three children mentioned above, by reason of their exploits, were ultimately called gods through the simplicity and folly of the common people. This was despite the fact that they were mortal and <term>corruptible|Subject to decay or death; not eternal.</term> men. For Jupiter was called the god of heaven, Neptune the god of the sea, and Pluto the god of hell. For this reason, the aforementioned Lady Ops received not only the title and honor of queen, but in addition to this, she was called and honored by the mouths of the simple, foolish, and unrefined men of those times as a noble goddess and the mother of the gods. And by public institution and ordinance, temples and altars were built and established for her, and priests ordained to perform sacrifices in her honor. <note>original: "Adeoque enorme malum" — And so great an evil...</note>

**A**nd truly, at the time when the Romans were in great need during the <term>Second Punic War|A massive conflict (218–201 BCE) where the Carthaginian general Hannibal famously crossed the Alps to attack Rome.</term> and the slaughter of the said Romans by Hannibal, this aforementioned execrable evil of idolatry and the worship of the said goddess took place not only in the land of Greece and throughout the land of Asia; but in addition to this, the said Romans—deceived by false and sacrilegious idolatry—decided to send messengers to the land of Asia to the King of that country, so that he might send them the idol and statue of the said Ops. They hoped that she would be a protectress and savior of the <term>commonwealth|The state or public welfare (Latin: res publica).</term> against the said Hannibal. And indeed, the ambassadors and messengers brought back to Rome a statue made of a very deformed and ugly stone, <note>This refers to the "Black Stone" of Pessinus, a meteorite representing the goddess Cybele (often identified with Ops), which was brought to Rome in 204 BCE.</note> which the Romans received with all reverence. And they had it placed and set within a high and magnificent temple as a goddess, believing she had the power to preserve and guard them from their enemies. And this statue was revered and honored for many years by the Romans and Italians before the time of the Christian religion. <note>original: "Mirabile profecto" — Truly a wonder.</note>

->B iij<- <note>This is a signature mark used by early printers to keep the pages of a book in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page explains the "Euhemerist" view that the gods Jupiter, Neptune, and Pluto were actually mortal men deified by common people, and describes how the Romans brought a sacred stone statue of Ops to Rome to seek protection from Hannibal.</summary>
<keywords>Ops, Saturn, Jupiter, Neptune, Pluto, Second Punic War, Hannibal, Idolatry, Roman Mythology, Euhemerism, Goddess Mother</keywords>

Truly, it is a marvelous trick of fortune, a great blindness of humans, and a wondrous deception by devils. By their work and suggestion, such a thing was possible: that a woman—one who suffered so much pain and toil in this world, and who has long since died and turned to ash, damned and lost in the depths of hell—could be regarded as a goddess and a saint. For so long, she received divine honors and sacrifices, which should be given only to God the Creator <note>The author is using a historical perspective called Euhemerism, which argued that the ancient gods were actually historical people who were later worshipped as divine by mistake or through demonic influence.</note>.

...knowledge until our own time. ¶ Truly from this <note>original Latin: "Verum ex hoc"</note>.
¶ Certainly, we can attribute the celebrated praises of the name and fame of the said Juno more to fortune than to any deed worthy of memory performed by her.
¶ For she was with Jupiter <note>original Latin: "fuit enim cum iove"</note>. ¶ Truly, the said Juno and Jupiter her brother—the children of Saturn and Ops <note>In Roman mythology, Saturn and Ops were the parents of the first generation of Olympian gods</note>—were delivered from the same womb. After this Juno was born, she was sent to an island in Greece named Samos, in which island she was very diligently raised during her childhood. Then, after she had reached the appropriate years for marriage, she was joined and wed to her said brother Jupiter, whom the ancients—in their simplicity and foolishness—reputed to be the god of heaven. This has been clearly testified by her statue and image, which for many years stood in the temple constructed and built for her on the said island of Samos. ¶ For, thinking... <note>original Latin: "Nam existimantes"</note>

For it was made entirely of very rich and excellent marble. <meta>This page begins a biographical account of Ceres, treated here as a historical figure rather than just a deity, consistent with the medieval tradition of "euhemerism" where myths were interpreted as historical events.</meta>

[Miniature: A woman in a landscape sowing seeds from a basket. There are buildings and trees in the background.]

**C**eres, as many authors agree, was a very ancient Queen of the Sicilians. She possessed a mind so clear and so clever that, after much deep reflection, she was the first to discover the method of cultivating and tilling the fields. She was the inventor of taming <note>original: "denter," referring to the domestication of animals</note> and castrating oxen, and of training them to carry the yoke.

She invented the use of the plow <term>plow: "cherue" in the original text, referring to the heavy agricultural tool</term>, the plowshare, and other tools required and necessary for farming. Once this was done, she broke up the fallow land and divided the earth into furrows. Then, she sowed seeds and grain into those furrows she had made and opened.

<note>original Latin: "Que cum," a common transitional phrase meaning 'And when...'</note>

When these grains and seeds had grown and ripened into ears of corn, she found ways to thresh, beat, winnow, and grind them, and finally how to turn them into dough and bread for the nourishment of people, whom she taught to do the same. For before this, people lived only on acorns, wild fruits, and apples from trees.

<note>original Latin: "Quod ob meritum," meaning 'Because of which merit'</note>

Because of this great benefit she performed and revealed to living mortals—even though she was a mortal woman herself—the simple and ignorant men of that time...

<summary>This page introduces Ceres as an ancient Queen of Sicily who revolutionized human life by inventing agriculture, animal domestication, and bread-making. It highlights the transition of humanity from a primitive diet of acorns to a civilized society based on farming.</summary>
<keywords>Ceres, Sicily, agriculture, invention of bread, oxen, plowing, domestication, Middle French manuscript, mythology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the goddess Ceres, shifting from the mythological abduction of her daughter Proserpine to the historical and social impact of agriculture on human civilization.</meta>

A single daughter named Proserpine, who was abducted by Neptune, king of the Molossians <note>The author uses "Neptune" and "Molossians" (a people of ancient Epirus) to provide a historical, rather than purely mythic, explanation for the story of Pluto and Persephone</note>, and was long sought by her mother Ceres. Of this Proserpine, the poets have written many great fictions and fables. ¶ There was besides... <note>original Latin: "fuit preterea"</note>

**W**ith the aforementioned lady, we also read of another Ceres, who was famous and renowned in the city of Eleusis in the land of Greece, in the region of the Athenians <note>original: "acticiens" (Attica)</note>. To whom Triptolemus was very obedient and subject, and he spread the knowledge of agriculture among the Greeks. It seems permissible to me to recite the virtues of these ladies under one and the same history, because the antiquity of the gentiles and pagans has honored and deified them with similar honors and sacrifices. And although the said ladies had a most celebrated intellect <term>engin: This Middle French term refers to innate talent, ingenuity, or mental capacity</term> and are worthy of memory for having thus discovered, increased, and augmented the science of agriculture; nevertheless, when I consider the time in which we now live, I do not know whether I should praise or blame their aforementioned invention. And it seems that one ought to praise them. For who would be the one to blame acorns being changed into grain <note>original: "blee"</note>, by which the body is nourished? Who would not extol the beauty of the earth when it is well-cleared, plowed, and cultivated, which previously was full of bushes, brambles, and thorns without any artificial beauty? Who would not praise the minds <term>engins: see previous note on "engin"</term> of men—previously rough and lazy—having been converted into very subtle, active, and contemplative ones? Who would not praise having reduced to a gentle nature those men living like beasts, who then were of a wild <note>original: "ferine", meaning beast-like or savage</note> nature? Who would be the one who would wish to blame the exercise of the physical strengths and virtues of men—who used to rest and be idle within pits and caverns—having been drawn out and called into cities and communities, both urban and rural? Considering that by the labor, diligence, and exercise of said men thus drawn together, so many beautiful and noble cities have been enlarged and newly constructed, so many noble kingdoms and empires increased and augmented, and so many civil and legal laws...

<summary>This page discusses the goddess Ceres and her role in introducing agriculture to humanity, arguing that this "invention" transformed humans from wild, cave-dwelling creatures into civilized citizens of organized society.</summary>
<keywords>Ceres, Proserpine, Triptolemus, agriculture, civilization, Eleusis, Attica, human nature, origins of law</keywords>

...manners and customs discovered and published for the governance and health of humans, and especially the science of farming wheat <term>fourmens: a term specifically for high-quality wheat used for bread</term> and other grains discovered and devised as has been said.

¶ **Which, though they are good in themselves** <note>original Latin: *Quae cum de se bona*</note>

Which things, although they are good in themselves, nevertheless according to the judgment of some, they would not be praiseworthy.

¶ **Finally, in practice** <note>original Latin: *Demum usatice*</note>

For who, on the contrary, would not praise the multitude of humans spread out and living in the forests, accustomed only to living on and using acorns, fruits, and apples from wild trees, and likewise using the milk of beasts, herbs, roots, and the waters of flowing rivers and running streams? Who would be the one to blame the fellowship and way of life of those men who were unburdened and delivered from all cares and anxieties, and content with the sole law of nature—sober, chaste, and without fraud? And who had no enemies, except only the cruel and devouring beasts? Who would praise them for having been drawn and called away from the use of the aforementioned foods toward soft, delicate, and unknown meats?

¶ **From which we ourselves** <note>original Latin: *E quibus nosmet*</note>

In which things men have deceived themselves, and we can see the misfortune into which we have fallen. For in doing what has been said—that is to say, by changing the way of life of the ancients—the sins which were hidden and concealed, and which feared coming into the light, have found an opening and a path, and have boldly taken to coming out and coming forward. Since that time have come the divisions of fields by markers and boundaries, and ditches have been made along with towns and cities; even the cares and anxieties of human labors have come forward. And these two words here:

¶ **"Mine and Thine"** <note>original Latin: *Meum et tuum*</note>

That is to say, "your thing" and "mine," which are enemies of public peace. There have also emerged private poverties and servitudes, disputes, hatreds, and cruel battles. Then arrived the most infinite pestilence of envy. And hardly were the curved scythes made and...

<summary>This page presents a philosophical argument contrasting the perceived "Golden Age" of hunter-gatherers with the corruption brought by agriculture and civilization. It suggests that the transition to farming and private property (the concept of 'Mine and Thine') introduced sin, war, and social inequality to humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Age, state of nature, agriculture, private property, Meum et Tuum, origins of sin, social decline</keywords>

...to harvest and reap the grain; instead, they had to forge and put iron to use to make glaives, swords, and daggers to shed human blood. And also, ships were built and framed to cross the sea from one country to another. From these things proceeded bodily softess and pleasures, the fatness of men's bellies, robes and rich ornaments to adorn human bodies, the splendid and delicate feasts of the tables, as well as laziness, idleness, and lust. This lust, which previously had not burned the hearts of men with burning concupiscence <note>Concupiscence: an intense desire or lust, often used in a theological sense to describe human inclination toward sin.</note>, then began to heat up beyond measure to the great harm of all living things. 

And it may happen in time to come—either through the corruption of the air or through wars—that such a great shortage of grain will occur that humans will be in great need and feel a harsh and cruel famine, the likes of which the ancients never felt as long as they inhabited the forests and lived only on the fruits of the trees. 

From this aforementioned way of life, so different from the life of the said ancients, have come and been caused sickness, thinness, paleness, debility, and staggering weakness, as well as the causes of many diseases which accelerate and provoke death.

¶ Having considered these things <note>original: "Quibus inspectis"</note>. All of these things, which I have observed well along with countless others, I can hardly express; yet I know well that the Golden Ages and times in which our aforementioned first ancient fathers lived—even though they were rough and wild—ought to be preferred and placed before our own centuries, times, and the Iron Age <note>The author is using the classical concept of the "Ages of Man," where the Golden Age was a lost paradise of simplicity and the Iron Age is the current period of greed and war.</note> in which we live now, however much our times are filled with pleasures and voluptuous health <note>original: "sanites." The author likely means "satieties" or "luxuries," contrasting the fake "health" of the rich with the natural vigor of the ancients.</note>.

# The ninth chapter treats of the Goddess Venus and begins in the text: <note>original Latin: "Venerunt ciprianam fuisse feminam"</note> "They say that Venus was a Cypriot woman."

[Miniature painting depicting four women in an interior room with windows. One woman stands on the left in a brown and blue gown, gesturing towards three other women on the right. **V**enus was lady of the Kingdom of Cyprus, of most excellent beauty. Some doubt who her parents were; but to further increase her beauty and nobility, some say she was the daughter of Jupiter and Dione.

¶ <note>original Latin: "Sane ex quocumque"</note> Indeed, from whomsoever...
¶ Certainly, from whatever father the said Venus may have been begotten, I have proposed to make mention of her by reason and cause of her sovereign beauty. For she was filled with such excellent beauty that those who had seen her could not be believed when they recounted her grace and physical **formosity** <note>Middle French 'formosite', referring to physical beauty and shapeliness</note>. And certainly some said that she was one of the seven planets <note>In the medieval worldview, the sun and moon were counted alongside the known planets as the "seven planets" orbiting the Earth</note> that we call Venus. Others said that she was a celestial woman who had descended from heaven. <meta>This page continues the biography of Venus, transitioning from her parentage to her cult following and her supposed historical actions as a mortal woman. In this medieval tradition, Venus is treated as a historical queen whose life was later mythologized.</meta>

...of mortal parents. And they affirmed her to be the mother of Cupid, whom the ignorant and simple pagans <note>original: "papans," a Middle French variation of "païens"</note> said was the god of love.

### Nor did they
<note>original Latin: "Nec illi"</note>

Certainly, she possessed many and diverse arts to deceive, through various gestures and bodily movements, the hearts of the fools who gazed upon her. Through the reputation of these arts, she came into such reverence that it was said she was the daughter of Jupiter and she was honored as a goddess.

### Nor only
<note>original Latin: "Nec solum"</note>

**T**radition holds that it was not only on the island of Paphos—a very ancient and celebrated stronghold of the Cypriots—that the ancient pagans made offerings and sacrifices of incense to her; but additionally, other nations, and especially the Romans, built temples and altars for her under the name and title of Venus. And they believed that an incestuous woman, now dead and reduced to ashes—who in her lifetime had prostituted herself and abandoned herself to carnal pleasure—would take delight after her death in having these sacrifices made to her.

### But as many
<note>original Latin: "Sed quod multa"</note>

**A**ccording to some, the aforementioned Venus married two husbands. And according to some, she was first the wife of Vulcan, the son of Jupiter; after the death of this Vulcan, she married Adonis, son of the King of Cyprus. After his death—either through the vice and corruption of her constitution <term>constitution: "complexion," referring to the medieval medical theory of bodily humors and temperament which governed a person's behavior</term> or the influence of the climate in that region—the said Venus fell into such great lustful desire that she tarnished all the brightness and nobility of her renown through nearly public acts of fornication.

### Finally
<note>original Latin: "Postremo"</note>

**T**o somewhat moderate her shame and remove some of the blush from her immodest brow—and so that she might give herself over to her carnal desires and shameful depravities with even more unbridled lust—she first invented the practice of brothels <term>brothels: "bordeaux," originally referring to small huts or cabins</term> and forced women to keep public houses to receive men. This custom has since come into use, not only among the Cypriots but also among other nations.

<summary>This page presents a "historical" account of Venus, reframing her not as a goddess but as a mortal queen whose scandalous life and marriages to Vulcan and Adonis led her to establish the first public brothels to hide her own shame.</summary>

# The tenth chapter treats of Isis, otherwise called Io
## And begins in the text: *Isis, who was formerly...* <note>original: "Ysis cui antea." This refers to the Latin version of Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris" (On Famous Women), where Isis is identified as the Greek figure Io.</note>

And that which he would not have dared to attempt or do against all his said nephews openly, he had done cruelly and treacherously by each of his said daughters. Furthermore, what he would not have dared to undertake by day, he willed and decreed should be done by night. Even what he would not have dared to do in battle, he had done in a secret chamber. He failed to consider that he was gathering as many years of bad reputation and infamy for his name—through the crime committed by him and his daughters—as those nephews might have lived in this world. He did not consider that, while by right he could have had fifty sons-in-law as good and loyal friends (whom he instead caused to be killed), one might escape who would rightfully become his mortal enemy.

There, by his ingenuity, force, and power, he occupied and won the kingdom of the Argives <note>The people of Argos.</note>, where he was finally killed by the husband of the said Hypermestra, his daughter.

And she married the most noble Amphion, King of Thebes, by whom she had seven sons and seven daughters. And truly, that which should have been a cause of joy for the said Lady Niobe became for her a source of sadness and a cause of death. 

For considering within herself that she was of such high and noble lineage, that she was a queen well-married entirely to her liking and desire to the said King Amphion, and that she was finally the mother of fourteen such noble and beautiful children, she became puffed up and prideful. And in effect, she wished to prevent the people of Thebes from making sacrifices to Latona <note>The Roman name for the goddess Leto</note>, mother of Apollo and Diana. This was despite the fact that the <term>divineress</term> <note>A female prophet or seer</note> Manto, daughter of the divine Tiresias, had announced and told the said Thebans that they should perform sacrifices and divine honors to the said Latona and her two children.

<summary>This page introduces the story of Niobe, a Queen of Thebes whose initial blessings—noble birth and fourteen children—lead to the destructive sin of pride. She begins her downfall by forbidding her subjects from honoring the goddess Latona.</summary>

# **Hypsipyle**

**H**ypsipyle <note>The text uses the spelling "Siphile."</note> was a very noble lady and the daughter of Thoas, King of the island of **Lemnos** <note>original: "lenuum"; the island of Lemnos in the Aegean Sea.</note>, at the time when the women of the said island had an unruly desire to exempt themselves and escape from the power and servitude of men. And to do this more easily, they assembled and called together all the women of the said island, including the said Hypsipyle, the King's daughter.

They took counsel and reached a conclusion among themselves that on the following night, each of the said women would slay and put to death her husband. And the said Hypsipyle even promised to do the same to her own father. Nevertheless, she did not do it, moved by pity and compassion toward her said father; for it seemed to her inhumane to soil and stain her hands with the blood of her own father.

And because she knew well that the others would carry out their intention, she revealed the matter to her father, who was old and ancient. And to save him, she put him in a ship in which he escaped the said peril. This done, she built a great pyre <term>pyramide de bois; literally a "pyramid of wood," referring to a funeral pyre</term> which she constructed by gathering wood together, pretending that she wished to burn the body of her said father and perform the final funeral rites <note>original: "derrenier exeque"</note> according to the custom of that time.

Which when... <note>original: "Quod cum"; a common Latin phrase used to transition between sections in medieval manuscripts.</note>

And because it seemed to the other women that she had dealt with her father as she showed through signs, and that she had truly and royally killed him, the other aforementioned women made her queen over them and seated her on her father’s royal throne.

Most holy. <note>original Latin: "Sanctissima." Referring to the sacred nature of filial devotion.</note>

And truly, there is no pity or compassion more holy than that which children have toward their parents; for there is nothing more fitting, more just, nor more praiseworthy. The 19th chapter treats of Medea and begins in the text: **"Medea, the most cruel example of ancient treachery."** <note>original Latin: "Medea seuissimum veteris perfidie."</note>

Medea was the daughter of Aeetes, the very noble king of the island of Colchis. She was beautiful enough in body, but far more instructed in the science of magic. For whoever had taught her, she knew the virtue and power of herbs so perfectly that no other could know them better. And furthermore, she knew how to trouble the air by her enchantments, to create winds and thunders, to compose poisons and venoms, and to do very marvelous things.

This woman and Jason. <note>original Latin: "Hec asone." A marker indicating the beginning of the narrative involving Jason and Medea.</note>

The aforementioned Medea was in love with the noble Jason, son of Aeson, against whom his uncle Pelias conceived envy because of his virtues. And so that this Jason might be slain and put to death, Pelias found a way to send the said Jason to the island of Colchis, giving him to understand that by force of arms he would conquer the Golden Fleece; to win which, many good knights had exposed their bodies to death. When the said Jason arrived at the house of the said King Aeetes, father of Medea, she cast her eyes upon him and was so struck by him that she proposed to make him her lover. And to better achieve her ends, she found a way to stir up her father’s subjects against him and to cause some sedition, <note>A rebellion or uprising against the king's authority.</note> so that Jason might have the time and opportunity to do and accomplish his desire. And certainly, there is no man who would not marvel to hear it said that by a single glance of the eye...

### The twentieth chapter treats of Arachne. And the text begins: Arachne, Asian and of the common people. <note>original Latin: "Arague asiatica atq; plebeia"</note>

**[A]**rachne <note>The text spells the name "Arague," following medieval French phonetic styles.</note> was a native of Asia, born of humble and lowly parents; for she was the daughter of Idmon <note>original: "hymon"; Idmon of Colophon was a famous dyer of purple.</note>, a dyer of wool. Even though she was born of lowly parents, she should nonetheless be extolled and elevated for certain deeds and merits worthy of praise.

¶ **They assert.** <note>original Latin: "Asserunt"</note> ¶ Some ancient authors say that the said lady first discovered the use and manner of working with flax <note>Linen production.</note>, and that she first devised and found the way to make nets to catch birds and fish. And because the son of the said Arachne first found the use of spindles <note>A weighted rod used for spinning wool or flax into thread.</note> suitable for spinning and weaving, some say that she held in her time the leadership and mastery of the art and science of weaving, as the most sovereign artisan to be found. Indeed, she was so expert in said art that a painter could not paint better upon a canvas whatever seemed good to him than the said Arachne could weave into cloths or fabrics everything she wished; which is a thing not to be looked down upon in a lady, however noble she may be.

¶ **Indeed.** <note>original Latin: "Sane"</note> ¶ Certainly, when the said Arachne had heard that she was famous and celebrated not only in small places, but also through noble and distinguished places and cities, she rose up in such great pride that she took the boldness to wish to strive against and compare herself to the goddess Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the Greek goddess of wisdom and crafts.</note>, the inventor of the said art of weaving.

¶ **And when she was surpassed.** <note>original Latin: "Et cũ se supari"</note> ¶ And as it happened that she could not patiently bear to be defeated and overcome by the said lady Pallas, she hanged herself out of grief, and through despair <note>original: "ipaciẽce" (impatience), which in this period often implied a spiritual failure to endure suffering.</note> ended her days miserably. And for this reason, the poets very appropriately feign <note>To "feign" here means to create a poetic allegory or myth.</note> that the said Arachne was converted and changed into a spider by the aforementioned Pallas; this is quite consistent with the name and office of the spider, which often hangs from a thread and weaves and makes its web just as the said Arachne used to do.

¶ Others say that although she put the noose around her neck to hang herself, nonetheless...

### The 22nd chapter treats Erythraea or Eriphila.
### And begins in the text: Erythraea or Eriphila. <note>original: "Erithea seu eriphila"</note>

**E**rythraea or Eriphila was a holy lady and one of the **Sibyls**, who was of very great and excellent nobility. Some say that these Sibyls were ten in number and they call and name each by her own name; but because all ten have most excellently prophesied of things concerning our faith and other authoritative matters, they call them all by the name of Sibyl; for "<term>Sibyl</term>" <note>The text provides a medieval etymology: "having or carrying God in her thought." In Greek, *Sios* (God) and *boule* (counsel).</note> means as much as having or carrying God in her thought. Among their number was Erythraea, of whom we now speak as the most celebrated and the clearest of all.

**And her [origins]:** <note>original: "Et eius"</note> The authors say that she was born in Babylon some time before the destruction of Troy; and others say she was born in the time of Romulus, king of the Romans. Some call her Eriphila, but she is called Erythraea because she lived for a long time on an island so named. In which island several of her prophecies were found.

**She was therefore:** <note>original: "fuit igitur"</note> And certainly the virtue and efficacy of her mind, and even the merit of her prayers and devotion, were so great before God that she deserved to have the spirit of prophecy and to predict so clearly the things to come that one could know them as clearly before they happened as after—this is apparent from the destruction of Troy, the Roman Empire, and what is more, she prophesied most clearly and openly of the **incarnation** and **birth**, **life**, **works**, **passion**, **resurrection**, and **ascension of Jesus Christ**. And also of his coming at the Final Judgment. So much so that she seems more to have told and composed a history than to have predicted and prophesied things to come.

**For which merits:** <note>original: "Per quibus meritis"</note>

### The 22nd chapter treats of Medusa. And it begins in the text: Medusa [was the daughter] of Phorcus, a most wealthy king. <note>original: "Medusa phorci ditissimi regis"</note>

*A miniature painting depicts Medusa as a contemporary medieval queen in a blue and gold gown. She gestures toward three men, illustrating her power and the effect of her presence within a royal court setting.*

**M**edusa was the daughter and heir of the very wealthy King Phorcus, from whom she inherited an extremely opulent and rich kingdom. She was of such great and marvelous beauty that she did not only exceed other women in beauty; rather, in addition to and beyond the beauty of others, nature had given her such a quality that she moved everyone who saw her to marvel at her aforementioned beauty. 

She had a headdress upon her head which was made of gold and precious stones, so rich and so precious that one could not imagine anything more beautiful. She had a face so pleasant that it seemed more angelic than human. She had a body made to be seen: tall and of such beautiful form that one could not see anything better shaped. 

But among all other things, the vigor and power of the gaze that came from her eyes was of such great efficacy that she rendered those she looked upon immobile, as if they had no memory of themselves. <note>This is a "rationalized" version of the myth where Medusa's ability to turn men to stone is explained as her beauty being so overwhelming that it stunned men into a trance-like state.</note>

¶ Furthermore. <note>original: "Preterea"</note> ¶ Some authors say that

She was very expert in the science of agriculture, and by means of this, she had a nickname and was called a Gorgon <term>Gorgon: derived from the Greek word for 'terrible' or 'loud-roaring,' though here the author links it to her agricultural skills</term>. ¶ Nevertheless, among the other nations, her fame flew even to the kingdom of the Argives <term>Argins: the people of Argos in Greece</term>, in which at that time lived a noble and courageous knight named Perseus. And he set out upon the sea in a ship upon which, for a sign, was painted a horse that had wings; and it had the name Pegasus because this ship ran upon the sea like a horse with incredible speed <note>This is an example of Euhemerism, a historical method where myths are explained as real historical events—here, the winged horse Pegasus is explained as a fast ship with a horse figurehead</note>. When he had arrived in the land of the said lady Medusa, he governed himself so prudently and did his business so well that, partly by love and partly by force of arms, he enjoyed the lady at his pleasure and took her treasures. He returned to his country charged with very rich opulence and a copious prey. And this is what the poets recite under a fiction, which can easily be understood by what has been said.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Hercules's captivity under Iole, following his conquest of her father's kingdom. It focuses on the subversion of his masculine identity as a form of psychological revenge.</meta>

...his beard, which was harsh and rough; and she placed a precious cap upon his head, and finally dressed him in soft and delicate garments of purple and other precious silk fabrics.

¶ *Thinking* <note>original: "Exestimans"</note>. The aforementioned Lady Iole considered that she gained more honor and was better avenged upon the said Hercules by having thus effeminated him and led him toward voluptuous things—he who was such a robust and virtuous man—than if she had killed him or put him to death by the sword or by poison.

¶ *Furthermore* <note>original: "Porro cum"</note>. And truly, the said Iole considered that she had not yet sufficiently satisfied the indignation of her heart, and that she would take even more revenge. For she constrained Hercules, through her blandishments and sweet persuasions, to appear in the clothing of a woman among the maidens and women of the said Iole. And the said Hercules had a <term>distaff (a tool for spinning wool)</term> at his side loaded with wool, and he sat among the said women, to whom he recounted and recited the stories of his <term>knightly deeds (valorous acts of a hero)</term> and virtues. And with the very fingers with which, while still in the cradle, he had killed and strangled the serpents, he now drew out the wool and spun with the spindle in his mature age. He sat in the middle of a group of common women to please the will of a young lady, who had thus conquered and <note>original: "suppedite" meaning to trample underfoot or subdue</note> overcome him.

¶ *Indeed* <note>original: "Equidem"</note>. And certainly, the aforementioned Lady Iole, through artful invention to her great honor and glory, avenged the death of her father—not by arms, but by subtle blandishments—to the great dishonor and shame of the said Hercules. Because of these events, he has incurred a stain upon his reputation that lasts even until today in perpetual memory. And truly, whatever glory the said Hercules had gained from the monsters he killed and from the father of the said Iole, it was stained and extinguished by the triumph and victory which the said Iole obtained over the said Hercules in the manner described above, making her own name worthy of eternal memory.

For this reason, the Romans kept the said books with all diligence and had recourse to them as if they were the response and revelation of the gods, for through them they had counsel on things to come. The said lady ended her days in the land of Sicily. And many years after her death, the people of that country showed her monument and tomb to those who wished to see it.

<meta>This page continues the biography of Carmentis (also known as Carmenta), a mythological prophetess credited with bringing culture to the early inhabitants of Italy. It concludes her story and introduces the next chapter on Procris.</meta>

...the Palatine <note>original: "palatinus". One of the seven hills of Rome where the earliest settlements were established.</note> on which mountain she had a place and dwelling built, where in the course of time the great city of Rome was made and constructed.

¶ **Truly Carmentis** <note>original Latin heading: "Sane carmentis".</note> ¶

And truly, it was seen by the said Carmentis that although the inhabitants of that place—who for a long time had been settled there through the gift and benefit of Saturn—possessed the knowledge and skill to cultivate and sow the lands. Nevertheless, seeing that they used Greek letters and that this was improper—considering they were outside the borders and region of Greece to be begging for letters and using those of foreigners—she provided for these letters through her wisdom and brilliance of mind <term>brilliance of mind</term><note>The original "clarte de engin" refers to her natural ingenuity and intellectual clarity.</note>, so much so that she invented some entirely different and unlike those of other nations, such as the Greeks and Hebrews. And after she had found sixteen—now twenty-one in number—she instructed men how to form and make these letter characters, teaching them how to assemble and join them according to the language of the Italians. And these letters we call Latin letters, to which certain Latin sages have since added six or seven without removing any of the ancient ones. 

And certainly for this we are greatly indebted to the said Carmentis. And by means of this benefit, the ancient men called her a goddess, and while she was living they celebrated her and performed divine honors and sacrifices. And after her death they built a temple for her near the mountain where she had lived. This temple the Romans—after their city had greatly increased and multiplied—did not want to destroy at all; instead, they named the gate of the city which they were forced by necessity to build in that place the Carmentine Gate <note>The *Porta Carmentalis* was a gate in the Servian Wall of Rome, located near the foot of the Capitoline Hill.</note>.

¶ **Many things of old** <note>original Latin heading: "Multis olim".</note> ¶

And certainly the land and country of Italy has flourished above all other nations and had renown for many things worthy of memory.

### The thirtieth chapter treats of Argia and begins in the text:

### Argia, a Greek woman of antiquity.
<note>original: "Argia greca mulier antiquis"</note>

**A**rgia in her time was a very noble lady who was born of the noble and ancient kings of the Argives <note>The people of Argos, a powerful city-state in ancient Greece</note>. She was the daughter of King Adrastus <term>9: a Latin-style abbreviation for "-us"</term>, and by her marvelous beauty she was desired and covetously watched by all the noble men of her time. And she has left to the ladies who have come after her, and those who will come, a whole and perpetual testimony of the love that ought to exist between married people; for these reasons, her name has come down in very bright and celebrated memory.

The said lady married Polynices <note>original: "polimites"</note>, son of the King of Thebes named Oedipus <note>original: "edippus"</note>. This Polynices, through the fraud and malice of his brother Eteocles <note>original: "ethioles"</note>, was ousted from the right he claimed in the said kingdom of Thebes. For his said brother broke and shattered the edict <note>A formal decree or agreement</note> by which it had been appointed that the said brothers would each reign for one year; while one reigned, the other would be absent and without government. 

The said Polynices summoned his brother Eteocles several times to depart from the kingdom and to let him reign in his turn according to the pacts and covenants previously made between them. In doing this, the said Eteocles was stalling; therefore, the said lady Argia made a request to her father Adrastus, King of the Argives, that it might please him to help her husband conquer the land of Thebes, which was his due and belonged to him as has been said. 

The said King Adrastus, complying with the prayer and tears of his daughter, assembled a great host of men-at-arms from the land of Greece, who besieged the said city of Thebes. Before the city there were several and diverse assaults, great murders, and killings on both sides.

### The 31st chapter treats Manto, and begins in the text: Manto, daughter of the great Tiresias.

**M**anto was the daughter of the very great diviner Tiresias, who reigned in Thebes during the time of Oedipus and of his children, the kings of the said place of Thebes. And certainly, the aforementioned lady was of such subtle and capable intellect <term>engin: innate wit or mental skill</term> that she learned so perfectly under her said father Tiresias the science of pyromancy <note>original: "piromancie" — the art of divination by fire</note>—discovered by the ancient Chaldeans or by Nimrod, as some wish to say—that in her time no one knew the said art more perfectly. For she was so expert in the said art that she knew—by I know not what diabolical work—how to divine and judge the coming of future things by the movement of the flames of the fire and their colors. And truly she saw so clearly into the entrails <note>original: "boyaux"</note> of ewes and sheep and other small beasts—even in the gizzards of birds and all other beasts—that by her enchantments and divinations it was believed she constrained diabolical spirits and damned souls to speak to her and respond to the things she inquired and asked about. And certainly, after the kings of the Argives <note>The "Seven Against Thebes" who attempted to seize the city.</note> who had besieged Thebes had fallen and tumbled to their deaths in battle, the said Manto, as some say, went into the land and region of Asia. ¶ <note>original: "Ad quos"—meaning "To whom."</note> To whom, after the said ladies had entered, they did not consume their time in weeping, nor in vain tears and talk; rather, they very quickly explained to their husbands what they had brought and decided. They passed by the prison guards very humbly with their faces lowered; and because of the reverence that one is accustomed to show noble ladies, and the darkness of the night which had already arrived, the said prisoners deceived the guards and went where they pleased. In their place, the ladies made no difficulty of remaining in the peril and danger of the same death and condemnation to which the noble men had already been sentenced.

### The 32nd chapter treats of Penthesilea / and begins in the text: <term>Penthesilea, Virgin of the Amazons</term> <note>original Latin: "Penthesilea Virgo amazonum"</note>.

**P**enthesilea was in her time a most celebrated Virgin and queen of the women called the Amazons—which is as much as to say, Queen of the ladies who have only one breast <note>The name "Amazon" was historically (and perhaps incorrectly) etymologized from the Greek "a-" (without) and "mazos" (breast), based on the legend that they cauterized one breast to better use a bow and arrow.</note>. And above all other queens she showed herself bold and marvelous through physical strength and discipline in matters concerning and regarding the feat of arms. And certainly she had a mind equally subtle / inventive / and very active for well executing her undertakings. She invented the use of battle-axes, which before then had been unknown to men. ¶ These things are pleasing <note>original Latin: "Hec st placet" — a scribal or editorial mark of approval for the text.</note>.

Since we should give credit to the writings of the greatest and most authentic authors, the aforementioned Pyrrhus <note>Also known as Neoptolemus, the son of the Greek hero Achilles. She possessed such firm and constant courage that, even though she saw Pyrrhus—driven by ill-intent—holding a naked sword to slay her, she never wavered in her constancy. And certainly, the marvel of her strong and virtuous courage moved the hearts of those present to admiration, just as the pity they felt seeing such a beautiful and noble lady die in this manner moved them to weep with compassion.

¶ Furthermore. <note>original: "Preterea"</note> ¶ Many painters of very noble wit <term>engin</term> <note>In this period, 'engin' referred to a person's innate creative talent, ingenuity, or mental power.</note> and almost all those of the time of the said Helen took similar pain and labor so that they could leave after their death, to those who would come after them, the likeness and image and resemblance of the said Helen and of her beauty. ¶ Among whom <note>original: "Quos inter"</note> ¶ Among which painters there was one more excellent than all the others, who was praised and paid a great sum of money to paint and render on a canvas the beauty of the said Helen; to do which thing he employed all the virtues of his wit. ¶ And as, besides Homer’s... <note>original: "Et cum preter homeri"</note> ¶ And as it was that the said painter of Heraclea <note>This refers to the famous Greek painter Zeuxis of Heraclea.</note> mentioned above had no model <term>exemplaire</term> <note>A physical reference or living person to copy.</note> to do this, except only common renown and the verses of the poet Homer to conceive in his thought and judge the beauty of the face of the said Helen and other parts of the body through the said verses and public fame. He thought that through the beauties of several other women—the most beautiful he could see and find—he could portray, paint, and draw the beauty of the said Helen and finally show what he had painted and portrayed. Which he did; for he chose from all the women and girls five of the most beautiful, worthy of being seen and looked upon for their excellent beauty. And by the liveliness of his wit he gathered and collected from the beauty and form of the said beautiful ladies all that he could, and portrayed and painted the image and figure of the said Helen as much as was possible for him through art. ¶ It is scarcely believed. <note>original: "Vix creditum est"</note> ¶ And certainly, it was not believed that by his said art and science <note>In the medieval sense, 'science' refers to mastery of a craft or specialized knowledge.</note> he could have fully perfected what he desired. And at this I am not marvelling. For who would be the one who could write or paint on canvas or wall the joyfulness of the said lady's eyes, all the pleasant affability of her mouth, her celestial laughter, and her decent and comely countenances, deeds, and words; considering that this belongs only to the office of nature. ¶ He did, therefore... <note>original: "Fecit igitur"</note>

...that her amorous cloisters were opened and watered with the delicious humor <note>The text uses "humor" in the medieval physiological sense, referring to the vital bodily fluids that were believed to govern health and fertility.</note> of a graceful nature, she was married to Menelaus, King of the Lacedaemonians <term>Lacedaemonians: the ancient name for the Spartans</term>, by whom she conceived an only daughter, very beautiful and pleasing, named Hermione.

**After these things.** <note>original: "Post hec"</note>

¶ A long time afterward, Paris, son of the King of Troy—who because of a certain dream which his mother Hecuba had dreamed <note>According to legend, Hecuba dreamed she gave birth to a flaming torch that set Troy on fire; this was interpreted as a prophecy that her son would destroy the city.</note> was exposed and placed in the forest of Ida <note>Mount Ida, near Troy. "Exposing" an infant in the wilderness was a common mythological trope to avoid a prophecy.</note>—which is near the city of Troy, and in which he was gathered up, nursed, and fed. And having later been recognized by his parents, Priam and Hecuba, [he was reinstated as a prince]. This Paris, considering the promise that Venus had made to him of the most beautiful lady in the world while he was in the said forest of Ida—or as others say, desiring to avenge himself and to seek back and recover his aunt Hesione, who had been abducted by the Greeks—had certain ships built and constructed in the said forest. And having done this, accompanied by several noble dukes and barons, he crossed the sea and sailed to Greece, where he was very honorably received and lodged by the said King Menelaus. 

After he had seen and gazed upon the great beauty of the said Helen—who was like a celestial being, noble, elegant, and arrayed in royal garments and ornaments, and desiring that her great beauty be seen and looked upon—the said Paris was suddenly struck by love for the lady. And because he perceived that she desired to be seen, he hoped that through her immodest character she would consent to his will. And he thought to himself that he would draw the said lady into the fire of his love through constant visitation and friendly familiarity, especially so that when he had fitting and opportune time to snatch and carry away the said lady, he would take her up and carry her off to Troy. 

**And fortune favors the undertaking.** <note>original: "Ceptisqʒ fortuna" (short for *Coeptisque annuit fortuna*)</note>

To this enterprise thus begun, as it is said, fortune gave aid and favor. For the said King Menelaus, for some of his business, left the said Paris in his house and went away to Crete. And during these events, the said Paris took a portion of the treasures of the said King Menelaus, and went by...

### The thirty-ninth chapter treats of Circe and begins in the text: 
### *Circe, by her incantations, even to this day...* <note>original Latin: "Circe incantacionibus suis in hodiernum"</note>

[A large miniature painting depicting a woman, Circe, standing on a path. She wears a long blue and gold gown and a tall headdress. To her left are several animals, including a dog and a pig. To her right, a man in armor is kneeling, holding a sword and a shield. In the background, there is a body of water with a ship and a castle on a distant shore.]

**C**irce, as the verses and writings of the poets attest and affirm, was a very famous woman and daughter of the Sun, who until this day, through her magic arts and enchantments, has had great fame and renown. And certainly, as I believe, the said Circe is called and named daughter of the Sun by the said poets because she shone in her time with most excellent beauty, or because she was highly educated in the <term>science</term> <note>In this medieval context, "science" refers to specialized knowledge, particularly of the natural world and the occult properties of herbs.</note>

<meta>This page continues the narrative following Ulysses' interaction with the sorceress Circe, specifically focusing on his departure after being well-instructed in his affairs.</meta>

...from the aforementioned Circe, well-advised and instructed in his business.

**Under which cover.** <note>original: "Quo sub cortice." This is a classic medieval metaphor: the "bark" (cortex/escorce) is the fictional surface of a story, while the "pith" or fruit inside is the hidden moral or historical truth.</note> Under the bark and the poetic fiction of these words, I believe it should be understood—as far as the actual truth <note>original: "royale verite." In this context, the author is seeking the "royal" or sovereign truth, likely referring to the historical reality behind the myth, a method called euhemerism.</note> is concerned—that the said Circe, as some say, was a very powerful woman who lived not far from a castle in the open countryside.

<summary>This passage moves from the literal myth of Circe to an allegorical interpretation, suggesting that the "sorceress" was actually a powerful historical woman living in a rural estate.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a description of a woman named Caieta, following a discussion of her location or origins on a specific mountain near the sea.</meta>

...called Caieta <note>In classical mythology, Caieta was the wet-nurse of Aeneas, but medieval and Renaissance authors often "rationalized" these figures into historical people to provide moral lessons.</note> and stayed on the aforementioned mountain near the shore of the sea. This aforementioned lady was beautiful and powerful in both deed and word; for she was such an eloquent speaker that she deceived everyone with her language. And she cared little for her honor, but instead prostituted herself to all strangers provided that they gave her gold and silver. And because of this, the lady, through her flatteries and beautifully ornate words, did not only attract those who already had the means to afford her desires, but she was also the reason many became thieves and sea pirates to seek out and give her what pleased her and to satisfy her greed. For she incited and encouraged many to abandon all honesty to pursue and engage in illicit and fraudulent trades and dealings; for this reason, the poets appropriately imagine... <note>The text likely continues to explain a mythological "fable" or metaphor created by poets to describe her behavior.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of King Picus and Circe before transitioning to the biography of Camilla, Queen of the Volscians. It maintains the 15th-century translator's habit of explaining mythological transformations as historical misunderstandings.</meta>

...thing suggests nothing else but that the said King Picus had a bird named after him, which is called a woodpecker <note>Middle French: "espec." This is a rationalization of the myth where Circe turns Picus into a woodpecker; the author suggests he simply used the bird for divination.</note>. By its song alone, he prophesied and divined things to come, governing his actions by the bird's movements and song. For this reason, the poets claim and say that he was transformed into a bird of that name.

¶ [Regarding when or in what manner she died] <note>Latin: "Quando seu quo genere"</note>
¶ I find no written record, nor do I have any memory of reading at what time, or where, or by what manner of death the lady Circe ended her days.

### The 40th chapter treats of Camilla, and begins in the original text: "Camilla, noble and worthy of memory." <note>Latin: "Camilla insignis et memoratu"</note>

[Miniature: A woman in a red and gold gown kneels in prayer before a statue on a tall column. In the background, a man (Metabus) stands on a riverbank, preparing to throw a spear across the water toward a castle on a hill. A small bundle, representing the infant Camilla, is attached to the spear.]

**Camilla** was in her time a noble virgin worthy of memory and queen of the **Volscians** <term>Volscians: an ancient Italic tribe often depicted as rivals to early Rome</term>. This lady was the cause of her mother’s death, for she passed away after the girl was born from the labor pains she suffered during childbirth. The mother was named **Casmilla**, and therefore as soon as

she had died, King Metabus, her father, removed one letter—specifically the **'s'**—from the name of his deceased wife and bestowed upon his daughter this name: **Camilla**. He did this so that the name would serve as a memory and consolation for his late wife, the mother of the said Camilla.

¶ [Of this, a certain...] <note>Latin: "Huius quidam"</note> ¶ And truly, Fortune was marvelously terrible and harsh to this virgin from the very beginning of her childhood; for soon after the passing of her...
